In these matters, one may *opine* — nay, be ignorant, saving the Christian faith. But **in things which are of faith, it is not enough to entertain some opinion** — the soul must be as fully persuaded as may be that what we hold is most true.

The reasons of this assertion are four:

**(i)** First, in matters of faith we are commanded not merely as it is in the Creed, but Scripture says: *He that cometh to God must believe that he is, and that he is a rewarder of them that diligently seek him* (Heb 11:6). And Paul writes: *with the heart man believeth unto righteousness* (Rom 10:10). Therefore *faith* is required, not opinion.

**(ii)** Second, faith has for its foundation the sacred Scripture, which does not admit of opinions but requires full assent: *thy Word is truth* (John 17:17). *Blessed are they that heard as unto a lamp that shineth in a dark place* (2 Pet 1:19). Who therefore believes nothing certain in matters of faith but only opines, calls into doubt the Scripture itself — that is, God's truth — which cannot be done with sound piety and unimpaired conscience.

**(iii)** Third, if opining were enough, our worship would never please God — for such opinions can never establish the conscience; and what is done with doubting conscience cannot please God.

**(iv)** Fourth, the heart of man must be certain in his faith, so that he may stand fast in temptations, which are not removed by opinions merely probable about God and divine things.

*Everyone therefore should see what he believes.* *For what simplicity is that of ignorance — not knowing what one believes?* (Jerome, *Contra Luciferianos*.) Every man should lay for the foundation of his faith some Word of Scripture from which he may frame a solid argument for his faith, whereon conscience may rest — and let him not care for reason's cavils, which offer nothing solid but only make the conscience scrupulous, which nothing obliges. Well then wrote Fulgentius (*Ad Donatum*): *Not all who are partakers of Christ, in responding also must express what they believe. But that also is a part of victory when someone cannot defend truth in speech, yet by heart avoids error.* Augustine's saying is well known: *A faithful heart is greatly helped, to know what is to be believed, even if disputing skill is not able to refute the contrary.*

### Case IV. Suppose the certitude of faith rests on Scripture as its solid principle, and faith comes by hearing of the Word of God (Rom 10:17). Are then all things which are heard from the doctors of the Church simply to be believed, without any contradiction whatever?

**Respondeo.** Modern **Enthusiasts** cast a scruple upon consciences here. Nicolaus Weigelius, in his little book *Dialogi Disputatoriorum*, vehemently urges that those who wish to learn celestial truth ought not to contradict their teachers, but as if in themselves dead and unfeeling, believe everything the teachers teach — meaning by "teachers" those of his own order, who boast internal revelations of the Spirit, and so under the name of *spirituals*, are puffed up in the School of the Holy Spirit.

And indeed the *simplicity of faith* which Paul commends (2 Cor 11:3) seems to require that we assent to our doctors without contradiction: for contradictions produce doubts, and make faith uncertain. On which account Christ says of the contradicting Pharisees, *my word hath no place in you* (John 8:37) — i.e., because of your contradictions. And indeed, in the recognition of truth, the Capernaites' were badly off, because they did not simply admit Christ's speech but murmured (John 6:60). Contrariwise, John says: *Beloved, believe not every spirit, but try the spirits whether they are of God* (1 John 4:1).

This scruple can be removed by a distinction of contradiction. Some is **licit**, and non-necessary in matters of faith — when for the sake of learning something is opposed to what is very difficult for us, especially where at first sight we have not yet heard some Word of Scripture on which our faith may rest. Here indeed it is lawful to inquire and object, provided the mind be brought docile — which from the Word seeks better instruction than it received, and no longer struggles but acquiesces in the Word.

Reasons for this assertion:

**1.** Nowhere has God commanded us to give immediate assent to our doctors in all things; rather he wills that we *prove all things* (1 Thess 5:21) and try the spirits whether they are of God (1 John 4:1). *Beware of false prophets* (Matt 7:15) — which cannot be done without contradiction.

**2.** The most holy men, whose authority in teaching was plainly divine, permitted themselves to be contradicted — how much more must the doctors of the Church admit contradictions and objections, whose authority is not to be compared with theirs. Christ himself the Pharisees and Sadducees contradicted, whose disputations on various matters he could tolerate (Matt 19:3, 22:23–28, 15:1–2; Mark 11:28); yet nowhere did he rebuke them for these oppositions. The Apostles Peter and John were contradicted in the Council at Jerusalem (Acts 4:7), which the Apostles did not blame but rather rendered account of their faith to the interrogators. The Apostle Paul, after his conversion, was contradicted by the Jews — whom he did indeed admit, but by responding confounded (Acts 9:22). Also Paul disputed in the synagogues (Acts 17:2, 18:19) at Thessalonica, Corinth, and Ephesus.

**3.** Even the angel in a plainly singular matter, which the human understanding could not grasp, admitted contradiction from the Blessed Virgin Mary: *How shall this be?* (Luke 1:34) — which contradiction, as Augustine expounds (Serm. 74), was not of doubting but of admiring, because she was wondering at the counsel of God, that from a virgin a mother should be made, from a human a God born. Such admiration can consist with faith, not removing but illustrating it.

**4.** No one can prohibit contradictions in matters of faith, because *faith is not the work of men but of God* (John 6:29), and *the Gospel is the power of God to salvation to everyone that believeth* (Rom 1:16). Nor is faith always and in all men equally firm, but often has many obstacles and doubts, which yet do not wholly remove it. *For my strength is made perfect in weakness* (2 Cor 12:9). To prohibit contradictions in matters of faith is to force consciences to faith not to persuade it but to impose — which no doctor of the Church ought to arrogate to himself. For not even Paul willed to *cast a snare* upon consciences (1 Cor 7:35), and Peter forbade the pastors to *lord it over the clergy* (1 Pet 5:3). God alone is Lord of consciences. For these causes contradictions of hearers are not to be forbidden — provided that in the manner of question of grace they are moderated, as those which rather augment than diminish faith.

Then there are certain **illicit** contradictions — disputations of reason pertinaciously opposed to the Word, which for that very reason is contradicted because reason cannot grasp the Word, nor will submit itself to the Word: such were those of the Capernaites, saying *This is a hard saying — who can hear it?* (John 6:60), and Nicodemus's too gross retorting to Christ (John 3:4). Here reason forges contradictions in matters of faith, though it has no place — because reason in these matters is blind, and out of its principles opposes what Paul calls the *ἀντιθέσεις τῆς ψευδωνύμου γνώσεως* (1 Tim 6:20) — *the oppositions of falsely-called knowledge* — in which while it pertinaciously persists, it at last degenerates into obduration with blasphemy conjoined, which happened to the Jews contradicting in the synagogue (Acts 13:45) and to those in the School at Jerusalem contradicting Stephen (Acts 7:57), whence at last a manifest sin against the Holy Ghost. This is reason in the sacred a *mere raving*.

These are the hostile contradictions — not of learning, but of assailing and reclamation — which very greatly hurt faith, for they oppose to the divine Word for other obstacles of reason, which Paul calls *ζητήσεις* — *disputes* — which simply oppose faith (Rom 14:10).

Hence there is a *manner* of contradiction: and since about matters of faith from the Word of God alone something certain can be taught and learned, let the pious mind be content — and be rightly instructed by the Word, though reason should always have something to bark. As the ancient distich has it:

> *In matters, wherein certain limits stand,*
> *Beyond which endures nothing right.*

From immoderate oppositions, at last, come contentions, which Paul calls *carnal* (1 Cor 3:3); and elsewhere he writes that the Church is *not contentious* (1 Cor 11:16); *nor let anything be done through strife* (Phil 2:3); *for contention is of those who are proud and know nothing* (1 Tim 6:4).

### Case V. Whether it suffices the believer to hold an *implicit* faith of God and divine things

*Implicit faith is the faith of the Church*, to which Papists ascribe very much, though even in this question they do not agree in all things among themselves.

By the *Church* they understand their prelates — or rather, the sole Pope, into whom every judgment of the Church is resolved. For as Bellarmine writes (*De Romano Pontifice* iv, c. 3): *That infallibility is not in the college of Cardinals, or in a council of Bishops, but in the Pope alone.* He who therefore simply believes what the Pope proposes to the Church to be believed — although he does not know what he believes — has the faith of the Church, or implicit faith, which by others is called *carbonaria* (charcoal-burner's faith), which Hosius Cardinal (*Adversus Prolegomena Brentii* iii) so commends that he calls it a *most safe asylum, in which more faithful help is found than in the exercise of all the Scriptures*.

Hence that blind obedience which their fathers commend to those bidden to believe. Those in the Roman Church, who are ordered to believe whatever they hear — though they do not understand, nor need to understand, but to depend on the labor of superiors, as ones half-witted. From this darkness not even the most keen-sighted and wisest of princes have they excluded. So writes Cassander (*De sensu Motu de ecclesia*): *the prince, in matters of religion, ought to be blind, and see with the eyes of prelates.* So in the Roman Catechism the first question is: *What is a Catholic Christian?* And it is answered: *He who believes whatever the Roman Catholic Church believes, whether written in the Bibles or not.* And even now from those who cling stubbornly to the papacy, though they can defend it with no pretext, it is frequently heard that they *believe what the Church believes*: which is that veil laid over their eyes lest they should acknowledge the truth openly shown. If we fall to the utmost, they impute all fault to the prelates of the Church, from which they suppose themselves to be immune.

**But such faith attracts ruin to many, for the conscience by no means can rest, unless one has known most exactly what he ought to believe of God and divine things.**

**1.** First, the sacred letters require of every man his proper faith: *the just shall live by his faith* (Hab 2:4) — which is as true of justifying faith as of the persuasion of things to be believed. For as no one lives by another's soul, no one is prudent by another's soul, but each is wise by his own soul — so no one can be faithful by another's faith, but each by his own.

**2.** Then Scripture bids us not simply to depend on the labor of doctors, but to *try the spirits whether they are of God* (1 John 4:1); to *beware of false prophets* (Matt 7:15) — which cannot be done without discussion of the proposed doctrine. Hence Basil in his *Ethics* writes (rule 72): *Subjects, who are trained in the Scriptures, ought to prove those things which are said by the doctors, and to receive those things which are consonant with the Scriptures, but to reject the foreign — and vehemently to oppose those who persist in such foreign doctrines.*

**3.** It is altogether dangerous to commit faith — on which the salvation of our souls depends — to the judgments of other men, and never seriously to inquire into it oneself. For unless it be certain that the Church to which you trust does not err, nowhere safely will your conscience rest in temptations. Ever the mind will return: *What if the faith of the Church you profess is false? What if the prelates of the Church be false-doctors, whom Christ bid us beware?* Necessary therefore is discussion and examination of the faith which the Church professes — which cannot be drawn from any other source than from *the Prophetic and Apostolic Scriptures*, for upon them the Apostle writes the Church to be founded (Eph 2:20).

**4.** The Berhoeans would not believe what Paul believed, although he was an Apostle indeed — but *searched the Scriptures* whether these things were so (Acts 17:11). How then would anyone without further examination believe the prelates of the Romans, who are not to be compared with the Apostle Paul? *Simplicity of faith* indeed has its praise, but *prudence* Christ also requires (Matt 10:16), *because many false prophets have gone out into the world* (1 John 4:1).

**5.** All are commanded to be *fully persuaded in their own mind* of what they believe (Rom 14:5); all are to *examine themselves whether they be in the faith, and whether Christ Jesus be in them* (2 Cor 13:5); all are to be *ready always to give an answer to every man that asketh a reason of the hope that is in them* (1 Pet 3:15). How can these Apostolic precepts have place in those who see only with foreign eyes, ignorant themselves of what they believe?

**6.** Implicit faith takes away all care of the young. What is the use, then, of laboring at the Catechism, of teaching the ignorant, that each may hold his own faith, if it suffices to believe simply what the Church believes, even in ignorance of what she believes?

*Objection of Gregor. de Valentia* (in his book on Faith c. 15): It is nothing (as Augustine writes) but the authority of the Catholic Church by which one comes to wisdom. And this Catholic authority is greater than the authority of the sectarians — therefore this rather than that ought to receive faith. He illustrates this by the example of a prudent artisan, merchant, or nobleman who, if he prefers the Catholic Church to the sectarians on account of the greater authority of Catholics, God will have nothing to lay to his charge at that dread tribunal against this unlettered man — for as by inquiring into doctrine to come to the knowledge of truth, God did not require of him, since he did not understand theological controversies. So Gregory of Valencia disputes for his implicit faith.

But in fact, *he falsely proposes the external authority of the Church for the inquisition of truth*. For in temptations the conscience is not established by any authority of men, but by God's Word alone; men can lie and deceive, but God is very truth and never fails. So Christ, sending the Jews as it were to a crossroads — whether they should follow the Pharisees or Christ — does *not* refer them to the external authority of the Pharisaic Church (which then was greater than Christ's), but bids them *search the Scriptures* which testify of him (John 5:39).

**As for Augustine** — he speaks of the miraculous nativity, operation, death, and resurrection of Christ; this he calls a most celebrated authority, which moves men to hasten to wisdom. But nothing does he say of the authority of men in the Church, as appears from the context.

Wonderful it is, indeed, that God at his dread tribunal should have nothing to answer to a man setting up merely human authority against his Word, as if from a tripod rashly. Christ did *not* say: *If ye follow greater authority of men.* But: *If ye continue in my Word, then are ye my disciples indeed* (John 8:31). And not by the authority of men but by his Word will he judge in that dread day, as it is written: *The Word which I have spoken, the same shall judge him in the last day* (John 12:48). It remains to our conclusion that the *implicit faith* of the Romans does not suffice for salvation.

### Case VI. Whether therefore the simple layman is bound to know all and each of the articles of faith explicitly

**Respondeo.** In this question the scholastics disagree. Some think it suffices, if the private and rude person implicitly and in general believes whatever the Church believes, though he believe nothing at all explicitly and specifically — as Azor holds (*Institutiones morales* viii, c. 6, q. 1).

Others deem it sufficient if the laity explicitly believe there is one God, rewarder of all the just and of good works, and avenger of the evil, while other articles are believed implicitly — which is the opinion of Hostiensis, Johannes Andreas, and Panormitanus (*De summa Trinitate et fide Catholica*).

Others hold that laymen ought to believe explicitly the articles of faith, at least those which the Church publicly celebrates, proposes, and teaches — such as the article of the most sacred Trinity, the articles concerning the human nature of Christ our Lord (*conceived by the Holy Spirit, born of the Virgin Mary, suffered, was crucified, dead, buried, rose again from the dead, ascended into heaven*) — which is Bonaventure's, Durandus's, and is called the *common* opinion.

Still others contend that any lay person, even the rude and unlearned, ought to know the Apostles' Creed. Others think that such laymen cannot be compelled to learn the Apostles' Creed, and that it suffices if they know the articles of faith as proposed by the Theologians — in this later opinion is Azor (*Instit. morales* viii, c. 6, q. 4).

There are also those who think that if one be of a very hebetic and obtuse ingenuity, it suffices to believe explicitly the clearer articles, and to believe implicitly the rest which he cannot attain — believing what the Church believes; e.g., one who cannot perceive the article of the Trinity, it would suffice him to believe explicitly Christ the Son of God, born of the Virgin Mary, suffered, died, etc.

**We judge that whatever things are necessary to salvation to be obtained or preserved must be known even by the rude, unlearned, and unskilled laity.** For no man can have care of his own salvation who is ignorant of the principles and means of salvation. And these are comprised in the *Catechetical doctrine*, which the Apostle touches (Heb 6:1–2), where he enumerates its chief heads: *repentance from dead works* (which pertains to the Decalogue); *faith toward God*; *the doctrine of baptisms*; *laying on of hands* (i.e., the benediction of the newly-baptized); *resurrection of the dead*; *and eternal judgment*. This doctrine he calls the *elements of the beginning of the oracles of God* — to be proposed to the simpler folk who *have need of milk, not of strong meat* (Heb 5:12); and he calls it *the word of the beginning of Christ, and the foundation* (Heb 6:1). Necessary therefore he judged that the rude be exercised in this doctrine.

But since not every one has the same *ingenious capacity* or occasion of learning, distinction must be made here between the *substance* of the articles of faith, and the *disputations* thereupon which have accrued for the sake of explication. The substance of the articles of faith every layman ought to and can hold; but the disputations and explications are not required of all.

*What is the substance of the articles of faith?* It is that which simply — though generally and confusedly by the words of the article — is signified: e.g., that God the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are not three Gods, but one God in three persons; if one believes this in general, it suffices, though he cannot say specifically what a *person* is, what the personal properties are, and how these differ among themselves — for these things pertain to the schools of the learned, and to the clearer explication of this article. So it suffices for the rude and unskilled man to know that in Christ are two natures, human and divine; that the Son of the Most High is called the Son of Mary; that the Lord of glory was crucified — all which Scripture says of Christ in concept-words, though he does not exactly understand the mystery of the personal union and of the communication of idioms, nor grasps what is disputed of them — for these too pertain to the schools of the learned. And so of the rest.

Then, many things are implicitly believed by the rude which are not expressed in explicit words of faith — which suffices, provided they do not expressly contradict. So he who believes God to be omnipotent and his Father, believes also that he cares for human affairs, and that not only by power and might but also *by his essence* is present to us. He who acknowledges the perfection of the Decalogue, implicitly confesses also the corruption of his nature; for the Decalogue also forbids interior concupiscence of the heart. He who hears in baptism that we are regenerated does not deny that we are born in sins — for regeneration is of those born in sins.

Since these things are so, necessity demands that every householder in his house, and every parish priest in his parish, diligently inculcate the catechetical doctrine to the ruder ones — which those who ignore are in great danger; and they will suffer penalties of their negligence who neglect those committed to their care in this respect.

Great was the diligence used in this heretofore, when the ruder ones, especially catechumens, on the day they were to be baptized were held to publicly recite the Creed and the Lord's Prayer — as Augustine testifies (*Sermon 42 ex quinquaginta*). And in the *Consecratio* (dist. 4, c. 41) it says: *Before all things, are recited words from another sermon of St. Augustine*, in which he exhorts sponsors that *before all things they hold the Creed and the Lord's Prayer, and inculcate the same to those whom they took up from the sacred font.* In the Council of Foroiuliense under Charles Pius, it was decreed: *Every Christian ought to hold the Creed and the Lord's Prayer as a memorized rule of faith* — *both sexes and every condition, male, female, young, old, servants, freemen, boys, unmarried, married, wife, and virgin, who by this hereditary rule cannot come into any part of the celestial kingdom.* Hence in our times, even in the villages, public catechetical examinations are held. And Luther everywhere, especially in the preface to the Small Catechism, urges this exercise — so that he holds those who refuse to learn the catechetical doctrine as apostates who deny Christ, and are therefore not to be admitted to the Sacred Supper, nor to sponsorship at baptism, nor to any part of the office of Christ.

### Case VII. What if someone through defect of faithful instruction and of the articles is entirely ignorant — is his hope of salvation not utterly despaired?

**Respondeo.** It may happen that some are wholly without care or knowledge of divine things, either because they were ill-educated, orphaned early of their parents, or utterly neglected by them, or because they grew up in fields, forests, or among cattle, or even from earliest childhood dwelt among barbarians and men estranged from the Christian faith. Their ignorance some think to be without sin and *invincible*, as they call it — in which opinion is Jesuit Azor (*Instit. moral.* viii, c. 3, q. 3).

**But if we wish to follow the sound rule of the divine Word, we cannot subscribe to that opinion.** For Christ our Saviour places eternal life in the knowledge of himself and of the heavenly Father (John 17:3); God the Father also places our righteousness in the knowledge of his Son (Isa 53:11). *Whoever, then, is without Christ, ignores also the Father* — according to that word: *He that honoureth not the Son, honoureth not the Father which hath sent him* (John 5:23; cf. John 5:15). How then can they have hope of eternal life?

**Nor does it help them that they received Baptism.** For in Baptism they were indeed illuminated, so that infants are said to *know the Father* (1 John 2:14) — but Baptism, through subsequent malice (which is connatural to man and is not corrected except through right education), is *evacuated*; and that illumination, through carelessness and neglect, is *extinguished*; and the discreet faith which is required of the adult is impeded. For this last is not acquired in an ordinary manner except by the Word — while it is read, heard, or by assiduous meditation is renewed. But such never hear the Word, and while they live among cattle, can scarcely have any notice of the Word; meanwhile malice grows, and long habit gives them either neglect or contempt of the Word.

Those who dwell among barbarians and men estranged from Christ, while they never hear anything of the Christian faith, are educated in the profanity of barbarians — and though born of Christian parents, they at last become enemies of the Christian faith. For them indeed in this state hope of salvation cannot be settled, until God takes mercy on them, and grants them also means of salvation.

**Wrongly therefore do parents and heads of households act**, who so accustom their children and domestics *from earliest years, as is said, from the fingernails*, to cattle — either sending them to pasture or drawing them into it — that for many years they scarcely once enter a church, they never see the schools; whence they draw upon themselves peculiar ignorance in those things which concern the salvation of the soul, whose blame largely redounds upon the parents and heads of households themselves.

The misery of **captive infants** is by far greatest, though no fault of their own — for whom therefore we must pray, that God free them from that yoke and lead them to the knowledge of truth: otherwise, entangled in the blasphemies of the barbarian foes of the Christian name, they have nothing that can promise them salvation, so long as they live outside the Church, and at the beck of enemies of the Church.

### Case VIII. Have the infants of Christians true knowledge of the true God?

**Respondeo.** The decision of this case has great weight for the untimely death of infants. For if it is settled to parents that infants have known God, they need not doubt of their salvation — which consists in the knowledge of the true God.

**That the infants of Christian parents know God, sacred Scripture affirms in express words.** *I write unto you, little children, because ye have known the Father* (1 John 2:14). By which place, that *little children* (who have not yet reached the years of discretion) are understood, is proved from the very order which John establishes — for he speaks separately of *fathers*, separately of *young men*, and lastly separately of *little children*.

In Greek they are called *παιδία* — such as the Greeks are children who need a nurse, by whom they are borne and cared for. Elsewhere they are called *βρέφη* — as Christ's *bringing children* (*infantes*) means such. Such infants in baptism are truly regenerated, and are adopted into the sons of God — which regeneration and adoption cannot be without the knowledge of the heavenly Father. For it is absurd to be adopted by one of whom one wholly knows nothing.

For this reason also Christ says infants are *believing* (Matt 18:6; Mark 9:42). But faith is not without knowledge. So whoever has saving faith must also know God, since in the knowledge of God salvation and eternal life consist. Little children are said to *receive the kingdom of God* (Mark 10:15; Luke 18:17) — which also cannot be without the knowledge of God. *Out of the mouths of babes and sucklings God is praised* (Ps 8:2; Matt 21:16) — but how can they praise him who is utterly unknown? Moreover they *please God* (Matt 19:5) — but *without faith it is impossible to please him* (Heb 11:6).

Infants therefore have *actual faith* from the operation of the Holy Spirit through baptism, which after its own manner (although to us incomprehensible) works in them true recognition of God and apprehension of Christ. For one is the saving faith, which consists in apprehension of Christ presupposing the true recognition of God. But it stands otherwise in infants than in adults. In adults, it is kindled by hearing of the Word (Rom 10:17); in infants, it is given in Baptism. In adults, it is *discrete* and with use of reason, so that they can distinctly discourse, meditate, and speak of matters of faith; in infants, it is *indiscrete*, yet true. In adults, it is put forth by external confession; in infants, this is not done, but the grace of God is to be believed to fill them, so that (as Augustine says, *De baptismo* iv, c. 14) they can neither believe with the heart unto righteousness (as adults) nor confess with the mouth unto salvation. Adults know that they believe and can prove themselves whether they be in faith, which is not given to infants; in which sense Augustine writes (Epistle 57 to Dardanus) that *the regenerate through age cannot yet know God with discreet knowledge, that is, cannot exactly consider it in life.* We do not deny knowledge to infants; but as they are *sanctified without sense of sanctity* (as Nazianzen speaks in Or. 40 *De Baptismo*), so also they can know God after their manner and believe with sufficient faith. For (says Augustine, Epistle 57 to Dardanus) *they know the Holy Spirit though he is in them, as they know neither their mind nor even their life — nor is it therefore to be conceded that they have neither mind nor life.*

**In sleepers, mentally-captured, agonizing, in the greatest temptations** — many think they do not know God, yet in them faith or the knowledge of God is not wholly extinguished, but in its time, the paroxysm bearing witness, puts itself forth. Even if therefore neither infants themselves, nor we, understand how they think or know God — yet not thence is the possibility to be denied them. For in **John** God worked notice of himself, whence for joy in his mother's womb he leaped, because he recognized his Lord present (Luke 1:41); and yet neither he himself nor we can express the reason or manner of that knowledge in the little infant John. We conclude therefore with Luther (from a certain letter to the Pastors on Anabaptism): *Undoubted it is that infants when baptized have faith, nor from Scripture can the contrary be proved, although the ratio or how that faith works is uncertain to us.* And in the *Formula Concordiae* between the Theologians of Saxony and upper Germany (year 1536) it was decreed that we believe infants — not indeed that they understand or feel the manner of faith as adults do — nevertheless are not destitute of every action or operation of the Holy Spirit (as Chemnitz refers, *Examen Concilii Tridentini* pt. 1, fol. 30b).

### Case IX. Whether the gentiles from the light of nature can have true knowledge of the true God

**Respondeo.** We defend the **affirmative**, as we shall shortly prove. But there are those today who deem this doctrine impious — a judgment which arises from either crass ignorance or shameful malice. The terms therefore are to be explained: what we mean by *true God* and by *true knowledge*.

By *true God* we do not understand some idol (as calumniators say the gentiles' conceptions of God were conceptions of an idol, not of the true God), but that true God who has revealed himself in his Word. Of this true God the ethnic peoples had some knowledge from the light of nature — not indeed *what* he is (namely Father, Son, and Holy Spirit — for this is learned from the Word alone), but *that* he is, and *of what sort* he is. For that there is a God — that he is good, just, veracious, omnipotent — this even the ethnics could naturally know:

- *First*, from the testimony of their own heart and the works of conscience, which when it accuses for evil deeds, terrifies, and lashes a man, testifies indeed that God is, who justly judges and averts sins: whence Paul says *the work of the law is written in the hearts of the gentiles* (Rom 2:15).
- *Second*, from the works of creation, which testify of the excellent Maker of all things. Whence Scripture speaks (Rom 1:19; Isa 40:26; Acts 14:17; Wisd 13:5; Sir 43:1; Ps 19:1; Zech 12:2), calling this *natural notice of God*. Beautifully has the poet said: *A footprint has been shown me by hands in the garden; the Sun demonstrates the divine numen to the beholder. It shines forth to me from herbage, from a hedge, from the outermost of the world. Whoso denies that God is, is not simply — as the peacock — without reason, but also without sense.*

This is *acquired* notice. Beautifully verses: *He who denies God is, let him look at the stars — for the Bear alone is enough to prove God to be. The pagan writers themselves confess their notice of God — so that Cicero says: There is no people so barbarous that would ignore God to be. Hermes said: God is all things — nor is any other thing besides God.*

Naturally is God's cognition weak and imperfect, not only because the precept concerning God — what he is — is wanting, whence the gentiles gravely erred in the application, attributing to creatures what were owed to the Creator — but also because they assent even to what they can know naturally of God, only feebly. Therefore this cognition can neither be salvific itself, nor lay a foundation for salvific knowledge.

Yet meanwhile it is *true* cognition of the true God, though it does not embrace all questions about the true God. For as in other things questions of *whether* and *what* are distinct from the question *of what sort*; e.g., the Patriarchs recognized the Messiah in the question of *whether* and *what*, but not in the question of *what kind* (namely, that it should be Jesus of Nazareth), because that person had not yet appeared in the flesh. So of the *Antichrist* — that he was to come, and what his works in the Temple of God would be, was known to many from Daniel and Paul — but that *this* would be Antichrist was not clear to all until the son of perdition was revealed. Thence the holy fathers on the question *what* concerning Antichrist sometimes seriously erred. So concerning *original sin*, and *what its effects are* — many things are agreed among the orthodox; yet many ignore *what it is*. As Sigebert against Luther wrote: The *fruits* of original sin in the Church are described, but *whence it is*, and *of what nature its effects are in us*, is uncertain. *What original sin is, and what constitutes its proper ratio*, the ecclesiastical definition has not made certain. Eodem modo in the question of God, that *what sort* God is, even from the light of nature has partly been established to gentiles; but *what* he is — namely Father, Son, and Holy Spirit — is learned only from the revelation of Scripture.

*That natural concept of the gentiles about God was not the concept of some idol, but of the true God, whom the gentiles worshiped*: this is expressly held from Acts 17:23: *Whom therefore ye ignorantly worship, him declare I unto you.* Paul certainly did not announce them an idol. Also because God *manifested* that knowledge to the gentiles (Rom 1:19); an idol could not be said to be manifested to them. Also because for that concept of God the gentiles are said to be *ἀναπολόγητοι* — inexcusable (Rom 1:20); for the concept of an idol they could not be so called. Also because of idols it cannot be said that they created heaven and earth, that they are good, just, chaste — such concepts of God the gentiles had, but they belong only to the true God. It remains, therefore, that the gentiles had some knowledge, not of an idol, but of the true God.

That cognition, though very languid, was **true** — where by us here the term *true* is not the same as *salvific* (as some calumniate), but the same as *certain*, congruous with the thing recognized; e.g., when they knew God to be omnipotent, veracious, just, etc., they had a *true* cognition of God — for all these things which they held of God are true: this *truth* Paul himself attributes to them, saying they *held the truth in unrighteousness* (Rom 1:18) — which came about in the false application of their true concepts, when they attributed to creatures what belongs to the true God (Rom 1:23).

When therefore we ascribe to the gentiles a natural knowledge of God, we do not treat of a perfect knowledge of God — such as does not fall even to the most holy in this life so clearly, such as is in the regenerate, whose natural light is illustrated by the Word; and much less of *salvific* knowledge, which is drawn only from the Word — but of a *knowledge of divinity, goodness, wisdom, power, justice*, and the like, which even Scripture attributes to the true God.

Such notice of God to the gentiles even without the Word, with good conscience we can attribute — because Paul himself attributed it: *inasmuch as they might have known God from the inspection of creatures* (Rom 1:20; Acts 14:17); he attributes to them *the law written in the heart* (Rom 2:14) — from which is collected the notice of the lawgiver.

### Case X. Whether they sin who deny gentiles from this true knowledge of God are put to salvation, so far as it can be

**Respondeo.** Certain of the Fathers seem to have looked in this direction, among whom is Chrysostom (Homily 39 on Romans, sub finem), who ascribes to gentiles a knowledge of God saved even before the coming of Christ — but this does not suffice, adding: *We suffer all things that are said to fall short, even those who did not know the incarnation of Christ, but abstained from idolatry, worshiped one God, and lived as well as they could* — and so forth. Clement of Alexandria (*Stromata* i) writes that *Philosophy of itself once justified the Greeks*. Others following, Erasmus (in *Tusculan Questions* on Cicero, being saved without faith); and Zwingli who *put Socrates, Seneca, Numa, and Pompeius in heaven*.

**But all these things (with all reverence for the authority of these men) are foreign to truth.** For Scripture ascribes salvation not to the external morals of men, nor to the sanctity of life, but to *that knowledge of God which is revealed in Scripture*, whose sum is that God is one in essence, three in persons — the Father, who begets the Son from his own essence, who was sent by him into the world to save what had perished; the Son begotten of the Father, born in time of a virgin mother, subjected to the law, that he might redeem those who were under the law; the Holy Spirit proceeding from the Father and the Son, sent into the hearts of the faithful, etc. Of this notice of the true God nothing certain was fixed even to the most wise ethnics. For though the majority of them acknowledged God to be one in essence, yet the *Trinity of persons*, and the *office of the Son Redeemer*, remained unknown to them. But *he who has not the Son, has neither the Father* (John 8:19; 1 John 2:12); *and whoever does not abide in the doctrine of Christ, has not God* (2 John 9). Whence Thomas rightly wrote (I q. 32): *By natural reason certain things about God can be known which pertain to the unity of essence, but not those which regard the distinction of persons.* And Armandus de Bello Visu (*De cognitione Dei* c. 14): *We come nearer to perfect cognition, which is God, by faith than by reason: for by faith we know God to be triune and one; to which by natural reason there is no way.* So he.

Other things indeed are read in *Mercurius Trismegistus* and the *Sibylline Books* concerning the most Holy Trinity, the Word of the Son of God, his eternal generation, incarnation, passion, and *baptismum*, and similar heads of Christian faith. Sybilline verse:

> [Greek verses on the birth of Christ and Trinity]

But such by many learned men are held suspect: they savor of more recent authors, and Christians, who under the name of the ancient Sophists in gentility set forth such things, that the pagans might more easily be convinced — an opinion Augustine himself takes (*De civitate Dei* xviii, c. 47). See at length here *Cardinal Bellarmine* from Casaubon, *Exercises against Baronius* n. 5.

**These things being so, they sin indeed who to the natural notice of God, which is in the gentiles, though languid, ascribe salvation** — not only because that cognition is languid and imperfect, but because they are destitute of *faith in the Saviour of the world*, on account of which cause Paul wrote: *the world through its wisdom knew not God* (1 Cor 1:21). And of the gentiles as *without God in the world* (Eph 2:12). Augustine likewise held the same, especially against Julian the Pelagian (*Against Julian* iv). And Luther, in a confession of his brief on this subject; Philip Melanchthon likewise (*Commentary on 1 Timothy* c. 6, part 2), and later in the *Examen ecclesiae Compendii Pelagiani* fol. 79, and in *Response to antithesis of Flacius* pag. 645 ff.

The **use** of this doctrine is to keep to the gentiles the possibility of salvation, in this that *God is truly the God of all* — that whom he wishes to have mercy on, whom he could justly damn, and nothing is revealed in his Word — and hence nothing about our soul can be feared.

### Case XI. On heretics and the dissimulation of faith in confession

**Respondeo.** There were once heretics called **Priscillianists**, who, that they might more easily conceal those things which their heresy set forth to be believed — some of them being base — hid their faith before others, using this precept: *Swear, forswear, but the secret betray not*, as Augustine relates (*De haeresibus, ad Quodvultdeum* c. 70). This from Catholics was imitated by not a few, to whom it was true that certain sad heresies must be silenced *until they can come to perfection*, as the same Augustine has (*Retractations* i, c. 60).

Others there are who think external confession less necessary — supposing it suffices that faith be in the heart before God alone, based on Ps 116 and Sir 14:21.

But to all these we oppose the express Scriptures which require external confession of faith of a man. So Christ (Matt 10:32): *Whosoever therefore shall confess me before men, him will I confess also before my Father which is in heaven. But whosoever shall deny me before men, him will I also deny before my Father which is in heaven.* Similar things see in Matt 8:38. So Paul writes (Rom 10:9–10): *If thou shalt confess with thy mouth the Lord Jesus, and shalt believe in thine heart, thou shalt be saved. For with the heart man believeth unto righteousness; and with the mouth confession is made unto salvation.* Finally Peter (1 Pet 3:15): *Be ready always to give an answer to every man that asketh you a reason of the hope that is in you, with meekness and fear.*

The **objection** of the *place of Paul* from Rom 14:22, is not about doctrinal faith, still less about saving — but according to its subject matter, it is about the notice of Christian liberty in adiaphora, which is to be concealed rather than seized to the scandal of a weaker neighbor; which makes nothing to the purpose.

**We say therefore: external confession of faith is necessary:** (i) because it is enjoined on us by the apostolic precept of Peter; (ii) because commended by Christ with the promise of a singular good; (iii) and because praised by Paul as a singular sign of true faith.

It is to be observed, however, the discrimination of divine precepts. Some are *negative*, which oblige always and to always — for at no time nor in any place is it lawful to do what God forbade, as *thou shalt not kill*, *thou shalt not steal* — and from the observance of these precepts man is never exempted. Others are *affirmative*, which do not oblige to all times — for it can happen that at a certain time, for necessity, one may lawfully abstain from their observance. So to hear the Word on the sabbath day is an affirmative precept, pertaining to sanctification; in a case of necessity, however — when the sick at home are to be cared for, or necessary journeys undertaken — a man is exempted from the observance of this law. So *to give alms, to pray, to visit the sick*, and the like, is a divine precept, yet not so necessary that it must be done at every hour and at every place.

To the affirmative precepts pertains **confession of faith**, which is therefore not always necessary. There are certain **cases where external confession of faith may be omitted, saving conscience**:

**1.** When one is in the midst of enemies of truth, and no one demands confession from him, and his silence hurts no one — while his confession would harm many. Then it is better to be silent, for it would be an untimely and rash confession, which God has enjoined upon no one — the way David, dwelling among the Philistines, uttered no confession of doctrine (1 Sam 21:11 ff.), and Esther in the palace of King Ahasuerus indicated neither her race nor religion (Esth 2:10).

**2.** When indeed the ratio of faith is demanded, but not seriously — rather by way of jest and mockery, as by scoundrels and fools who mock at Christian things. Here has place that saying: *Answer not a fool according to his folly, lest thou also be like unto him* (Prov 26:4). Ineptly, then, or otherwise beyond what is needed, no perpetual confession is required, which jactant rather has the *species* of Christian virtue than the *praise* — in which way indeed *the Apostles were always ready to give account of their faith to all who asked* (1 Pet 3:15).

**3.** When spontaneous confession draws danger against the doctrine itself — the way Christ *charged* the two blind men whom he had healed *to tell no man* (Matt 9:30), because this fact could increase the envy of the Pharisees and Scribes against him: which is what happened, so that Christ soon on the next day was forced to depart from Capernaum. Similarly the deaf-mute cured (Mark 7:36) — Christ forbade the publication of this miracle because he feared it would too much irritate his enemies.

**4.** When the doctrine has not been fully learned, and its confession before others is dangerous. On which account we do not permit those who have recently joined our churches to make their public confession, until they have been more established in the doctrine of the Church.

**5.** When confession would be an offense to the weak brother — for even Paul denied himself lawful things for the weak (Rom 14; 1 Cor 8), and Christ paid the tribute *lest we should offend them* (Matt 17:27), though the Son was free.

Beyond these, external confession is required — as when someone is asked by an infidel or heretic about his faith: not to dissimulate, but to open it — even at the peril of his life. For then is the time of confession and of probation; and by silence, truth is betrayed, and the enemies rejoice in the hope of consent. Here has place that saying of Christ: *He that denieth me before men*, etc. Silence in confession is denial of confession.

**We cannot therefore subscribe to the opinion of those who think that he does not sin who, asked by some private person whether he is a Christian, hides his faith — or evades the questioner with words indifferent, as Ambrose Catharinus holds (*Opusc. de necess.* p. 3, p. 5, p. 28, in a treatise on ambiguities). For an evasion is a species of lie. Better therefore is a plain colloquy — or altogether omitting the meeting with enemies, or manifestly rendering to any asking a reason of faith, at what time the ratio of the case bears it.** Rather is confession before the faithful required not only in words but in deed — as when one, dwelling among Papists, is bidden to interest in processions, to prostrate before the sacramental little chapel, to adore before statues; if he should do so, it would be a species of denial — therefore by intermission of the work our confession is to be given, whatever the peril.

**Summary — when to the impious an occasion is given for blaspheming God's Name, or to the godly an occasion for stumbling, by our silence — then confession is necessary: for then by our dissimulation the glory of God is diminished and the utility of the neighbor is impeded.**

**Note:** even where confession of one's own faith is not always necessary, it is always required not to simulate any sect or infidelity — for this latter belongs to the negative precepts which oblige always. So he sins gravely who takes on Jewish dress among the Jews or Turkish among the Turks in order to hide — for by such external habit he simulates the heresy of the Jews or the infidelity of the Turks, which is unlawful for a Christian: for even this simulation has the species of denial.

— ❦ —

## Chapter II. On the Knowledge of God with Respect to Essence and Persons

*De cognitione Dei quoad essentiam et personas.*

We have hitherto treated of faith and the knowledge of God *in himself*, but abstractly — apart from his essence, persons, and attributes. Now we come nearer to God, so far as he has revealed himself in his Word: *one in essence, three in persons*. And since the knowledge of God in this world is *speculative and enigmatic* (1 Cor 13:12), certain cases occur about this part of doctrine which disturb conscience — which the pious mind can meet with brief occasion, until a full and perfect knowledge of God attain us, such as eternal life shall follow.

### Case I. Whether one may without sin conceive of God under some corporeal form

Since God is Spirit (John 4:24), how can any corporeal substance be attributed to him? As some sinned in excess (Anthropomorphites who imagined God to have a bodily shape, whom Augustine reproves in *De haeresibus* c. 50), so others sin in defect — who make it a great sin that any man in praying or thinking of God should form some image of a venerable elder. Which the Calvinist party especially does, straining at a gnat and swallowing a camel. So writes Alsted in *Theologia Catechetica* p. 43: *We ought to form no conception of God in the mind, whereby we imagine God to be of a bulk or bodily figure.* And Perkins (*Cases of Conscience* i, c. 4, q. 3): *When we conceive of God in the human mind or in a corporeal figure, or in whatsoever similitude, however subtle, we do not rightly apprehend him, but rightly divide from him. Because it is nothing else than to imagine that God's substance is like a man's — which is one degree from a most gross imagination, and is not to conceive of God in this manner, but to be gradually dragged forth to a right conception.*

**Respondeo.** *Our conception can no way exhaust the essence of God, for God is infinite and incomprehensible* — but in his works (as by prophecy in the Word he has revealed himself) *he can be conceived as he can*. Hence Moses (Exod 33:23): *Thou shalt see my back parts: but my face shall not be seen.* Of this there is no doubt. But doubt arises whether *concepts of God under some corporeal form* can be entertained without sin.

Here we hold that the *hard opinion of the Calvinists* is to be rejected. For it is that very question by which they hold the consciences of the simple — whom Christ redeemed with his blood — though they be held for rude and stolid by the wise of this world.

**1.** There is no sin in such a concept, since without concept the human mind cannot even think of God. Whoever speaks of God or directs prayers to God, conceives some image. It is better to conceive the image of the name in which God himself appeared, than to conceive some other. There are, therefore, natural concepts of God innate in the mind, which not even those who insist against them can always change.

**2.** God himself has set himself before the minds of men under such a concept — when he appeared in the form of a man, in the form of an aged elder, in the form of an armed prince, whom the Prophet calls the *Ancient of Days* (Dan 7:9), in the form of a king seated upon the throne of glory (Gen 18:2; Josh 5:13 f.; Dan 7:9; Isa 6:1 ff.). These histories are not to be read by ordinates without such conceptions, and God willed to accommodate his infirmity to us in these external forms, because of God we can neither speak nor think except in the human mode. Nor is it to be feared that these concepts might comprehend the very incomprehensible essence of God — for no one is so obtuse as not to know that not the very *essence* of God, invisible in itself, is in that history subjected to the eyes of men.

**3.** The Calvinists themselves do not entirely refuse such corporeal concepts of God — for they too form for themselves a peculiar concept of God in the effigy of solar rays with the inscription "Jehovah," which concept has no foundation in Scripture whatsoever.

Therefore, no man ought to entertain a scruple in conscience if perhaps in prayer he thinks of God under the form of a man; we name our *Father* — why can we not think of him as of some elder? Only take heed that thou dost not under that human form conceive the divine essence itself.

**We conclude therefore that such concepts of God in the mind are without sin, provided they are such as are found in Scripture.** In Scripture God has (i) exhibited himself to the eyes of men in some human form because of the infirmity of men — therefore if anyone entertains such a conception of external form of God in his own soul, he is by no means said to sin; (ii) revealed himself in the Word as good, wise, just, omnipotent, creator of heaven and earth, redeemer of the human race, etc. — in these therefore his works and attributes we are to conceive of him, as he willed to be gladly known to us through his attributes (Exod 34:6; Jer 9:23; Dan 4 ff.); (iii) manifested himself in Scripture as Father, Son, and Holy Spirit — that is, one God in three persons; therefore God is not to be conceived apart from the three persons, nor ought Son and Holy Spirit to be excluded from him who thinks of God the Father, and vice versa: for these three are one. With these things observed, our concept of God will be conformed to Scripture.

### Case II. Whether without sin the image of God can be painted or sculpted, though not for cult

**Respondeo.** The Calvinists likewise raise this scruple to tender consciences, accusing all images of idolatry, whether in temples or private houses, even used only as ornament or for the sake of history. But this is a vain fear, provided one presupposes that the very essence of God — as it was never seen by any mortal — is neither painted nor sculpted nor in any way expressed nor subjected to our eyes. Here Isa 40:18 has its place: *To whom then will ye liken God? or what likeness will ye compare unto him?* Neither must any image of God be fashioned for our use, but this is properly forbidden — that we make for ourselves no image whose likeness we contain. But that is a *history* set forth before the eyes of men (as the historical books were bound with images, so also the Bible).

**1.** The Calvinists themselves entertain such corporeal concepts of God — since they attribute them to God, as is seen in Moses's book *Delitiae* p. 226. Nay, they themselves form a peculiar concept of God under the effigy of solar rays with the inscription of the name *Jehovah*, though such a concept has no foundation in Scripture.

**2.** God himself exhibited such an image of himself to be painted, when he appeared in that form.

**3.** The holy Patriarchs are held to have expressed at least the person of him who appeared to them: as concerning the Patriarch Jacob, Epiphanius testifies in the Greek chain.

**4.** The Calvinists themselves are without sin, they think, in using an image by which they represent God — namely in the form of solar rays with the inscription of the name *Jehovah*, and sometimes at the front of their books they suffer the image of God to be prefixed in the form of an elder, as may be seen in Zwingli's works, Beza's *Fasciculus*, and other of their works. On the frontispiece of the same books the image of God the Father appears with promised beard, and the Holy Spirit is beheld in the appearance of a dove.

**5.** We concede with Damascene (*De fide orth.* i, c. 4) that the images of the *angels themselves* cannot be painted, yet those visible forms which they sometimes take on can be painted (Gen 18 c., Josh 5, Judg 18, Isa 6 sqq.); why then can not the visible form of God in which he appeared be painted? For it is not to be feared that his intimate essence should be circumscribed by this picture — as if we thought so of the angels, for neither would their essence be described by their picture, but only their appearing form is represented, in which no one can sin.

**6.** Nowhere is it forbidden to paint God in the form in which he appeared. For as to the places of Exod 20:4 and Deut 4:15 — which are so often set forth — it is not on that account that they are to be objected to, for images are not simply forbidden there, but *idols*, because God forbids gods of gold or silver or of any melted matter to be made (Exod 20:23; Lev 19:4); why the sculpture is not to be made, is expressly added — namely, lest something in place of divinity be represented, or some form of divinity fashioned (Deut 4:16). So just as *thou shalt have no other gods before me* (Exod 20:3), so also *thou shalt not make thee molten gods to be with me* (v. 23). So in Exod 34:14 the reason is added, why altars are to be broken down.

We conclude therefore with Dom. Chemnitz (*Examen* pt. 4, fol. 21): *the highest and most certain and most excellent image of God and of Christ is that which our mind's cognition forms and conceives from the Word of God — but yet concerning things indifferent one should not fight bitterly.* For neither piety nor sin ought to be placed in this — provided no impious opinions or abuses be added. Since God himself in visions has shown such images, therefore just as it is not a sin to express such vision-histories with words, so also it is not a sin to draw those histories historically and to render them to the eye of the historical picture — provided only that this be fixed, that the very essence of divinity neither ought to be, nor can be, expressed by any figure or image.

### Case III. On the Trinity — against those who from the disputations concerning God disturb conscience (Photinians and Arians)

*Cum ex sacris literis testimonia luculenta de Trinitate personarum in unitate essentiae divinae haberi possint, in quibus conscientia adquiescere potest — inquisitur, an et quomodo ex Scriptura Sacra fides et cognitio Trinitatis stabiliri possit?*

**Respondeo.** For this scruple to be decided, and the pious man's conscience taught, three things must be shown:

**(i) That in the unity of God there is *plurality*.**
**(ii) That this plurality is *precisely three*, not more.**
**(iii) That these three are not three Gods but three persons in one God.**

**First — plurality in God — is shown from Scripture, in which God concerning himself is set forth in plural number:**

- *Let us make man in our image* (Gen 1:26).
- *Behold, the man is become as one of us* (Gen 3:22).
- *Let us go down, and there confound their language* (Gen 11:7).
- *I heard the voice of Jehovah, saying, Whom shall I send, and who will go for us?* (Isa 6:8).

Since God can neither think nor speak amiss concerning himself — for the pious mind cannot think of God, more reverently and rightly than as God himself has spoken — therefore if he speaks of himself as of several, we cannot err by acknowledging some plurality in God. So Hilary (*De Trinitate* v) approves: *It is not enough to be baptized with the Only-begotten, but only to be known with God.*

*Objection*: The great man might be speaking of himself in the plural — as Saul (1 Sam 16:20 f.), David (2 Sam 15:6), Solomon (1 Kgs 1:26), Ahab (1 Kgs 22:6). *Reply*: This mode of speaking is *Hebraism*, since in Scripture the writings of kings and princes are commonly published in the singular. If God had wished to imitate the custom of other peoples, doubtless he would have done so in the promulgation of the Decalogue, where above all he declared his majesty — but this is not so found there (an observation of Musculus on Genesis).

*Objection*: God might have been speaking to angels — *let us make, let us descend, one of us*. *Reply*: (i) In the work of creation, God says *let us make man in our image* — but man was not created in the image of angels (see Augustine, *De civitate Dei* xii, c. 6; Gen 3:22 on the comparison of man to God, not to angels); (ii) the devil in tempting man does not say *ye shall be as the angel*, but *ye shall be as God* (Gen 3:5), to which afterwards it alludes: *Behold, the man has become one of us* (v. 22) — pointing to plurality in God; (iii) at the judgment of the builders of the Tower of Babel — which is ascribed to God, who alone could confound the languages of men — as also he made the mouth of man, dumb and deaf (Exod 4:11).

Then plurality is proved in God by those sayings in which God speaks of God: *Jehovah rained fire from Jehovah* (Gen 19:24); *Have mercy on us, God our salvation* (Ps 65:5); *God said, I will save them in Jehovah their God* (Hos 1:7); *Jehovah said unto my Lord: sit thou at my right hand* (Ps 110:1). Whence a pious mind can gather: God concerning himself in one person cannot speak, but there must be another — one who speaks, another concerning whom he speaks; one who rains, another concerning whom it rains; one who saves, another to whom it is said, sit at my right hand.

**Third mark — Elohim.** In those places where the plural noun *Elohim* is joined with a singular verb without enallage or catachresis (as Gen 1:1: *In the beginning Elohim created the heaven and the earth*), the pious mind should hold the twin Hebrew rules: (i) *A plural noun with a singular verb, without enallage or catachresis, is put when several are truly signified*; (ii) *A plural noun with a singular verb signifies not distribution but plurality* — as *created the luminaries* (Gen 1:16), *the she-lion couches* (Prov 19:12), *the son cometh forth* (Gen 34:22), *the beast bellows* (Job 40:5). Just so in our saying *created Elohim*, that is, *each person of Deity created*. Hence Scripture says *thy Makers* (Isa 54:5; Ps 149:2).

*Objection*: The name *Elohim* has no plural signification, since it is added to a singular verb, so it is put catachrestically. *Reply*: If it were catachrestical, one would say *created Elohim*, *the luminaries shine*, *the she-lion couches*, *the son cometh forth* — but Hebrews speak elegantly, and not without reason: because they signify one thing among several distinctly. So we must recognize the very word *Elohim* used of the true God with a plural verb: *whence appears true plurality is noted* — Gen 20:13, Abraham says: *the Elohim caused me to wander from my father's house*; Gen 12:7 the Gods appeared to me; 1 Sam 4:7 the Gods came to redeem their people.

*Objection*: *Elohim* may be used of only one person — the Father is called Elohim, the Son is called Elohim, the Spirit is called Elohim (as Gen 1:2, the *Spirit of Elohim*). *Reply*: The name *Elohim*, since it is a plurality-noun by antonomasia, first belongs to the Trinity of persons joined together; nevertheless any one person retains this appellation secondarily, on account of the essence-unity which is between them. For no person is by itself God, but is God in the Trinity and with the other two. So the plural word *Elohim*, even when predicated of one person, has respect to the mystery of the Trinity.

**Second — that this plurality is precisely three, not more.** For the two-natured Elohim in the singular (Ps 114:7; Job 11:7; Isa 44:8; Ps 8:5) signifies God, but of plurality of persons. This is what proper names of God — *Jehovah, Jah, Shaddai* — do not have: since these note only the essence of God, which is one.

Since therefore mind is confirmed of plurality in God, second: this plurality is distinctly and precisely three, not more or fewer. And here again we must have recourse to Scripture, which shows three distinct in God:

- *By the Word of the Lord were the heavens made; and all the host of them by the breath of his mouth* (Ps 33:6). You hear here the Lord, you hear his Word, you hear his Spirit of the mouth.
- *And now the Lord God, and his Spirit, hath sent me* (Isa 48:16). You see again three in God — Jehovah who is sent (for through the whole chapter he speaks), and here says himself: Jehovah who sends, and the Holy Spirit likewise sending.
- *Similar is the place Isa 63:7–8, 9, 10*, where mention is made of *Jehovah, whose mercies are proclaimed* (v. 7), the *angel of the presence of Jehovah, who saved the people* (v. 9), and *the Holy Spirit, whom the people provoked* (v. 10). What can be clearer?

In the New Testament the illustrious appearance at Christ's baptism (Matt 3:16 f.): to which testimony Athanasius bids the Arians be sent back: *go to Jordan, that you may see and be silent* (as Augustine has it *Tractate on 1 John* — *there appeared the Trinity most manifestly: Father in the voice, Son in the man, Holy Spirit in the dove*). So we are commanded to baptize *in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit* (Matt 28:19). Hither pertains Christ's saying: *When the Comforter is come, whom I will send unto you from the Father, the Spirit of truth* (John 15:26). You hear here distinctly three in God: the Holy Spirit who is sent, the Son of God who sends, and the Father from whom he is sent. So in 1 John 5:7: *there are three that bear record in heaven, the Father, the Word, and the Holy Spirit.* These testimonies can suffice for the pious that in God are no more than three; further testimonies are found here and there.

**Third — that these three are not three Gods but three distinct persons.** That the three are not gods, is shown by the clear places of Scripture about the unity of God, which are everywhere at hand. That the three are not only three names but three distinct persons appears from those distinct operations and attributes — that the Father is heard in baptism, the Son is baptized, the Holy Spirit descends in the form of a dove; that the Son sends, the Spirit is sent, the Father is; that three distinct witnesses are numbered, and what are similar. Therefore it is settled from the sacred letters, that three in God are truly distinct — not by essence, not by species, not by name, but by *number*.

These three, first in the Church before the heresies of Praxeas and Sabellius were known, were called *without any peril* just *three names*, since in one substance they are the same. Not yet with the heretics disputing had the fathers spoken more cautiously. But when Sabellius openly taught that there was in the Deity only one person, which had three names, the orthodox Church called those three, truly distinct in God, "three properties of names." But since the heretics also played with this word, the Church at length used the name of *three persons*, that they might say *there are in one essence three persons*. Hence the term *Trinity* prevailed in the Church, because of the three persons in God. This word is used by Justin Martyr, who wrote a book *Peri Trisagios* (*ῥήματα τρίας*); Clement of Alexandria (*Stromata* iv) uses the word *τριάς*; Tertullian *Against Praxeas*, Origen and others often used the word *person* and *Trinity*.

**Trinity is not constituted by a plurality of essence, but by the diversity of characteristic properties — whence God is not called triple, but triune.** *Triple* is that which is composed of three; *triune* is one being of essence having three modes of existing in divine things.

Even though these words are not found in Scripture exactly in this sense throughout, and though in themselves they may have some imperfection, yet the pious mind may rest content in their use, since the Church could not express its faith against the heretics without these words. To which sentiment Augustine writes (*De Trinitate* vii, c. 4): *One God, Father, Son, and Holy Spirit — through this appellation we speak of the ineffable, as we can somehow say, what is unspeakable to somehow say. It is said by our Greeks: one essence, three substances, three persons.* Let us remember, however, that if any imperfection occur in these words, it is to be purged before these mysteries are approached — so it is not necessary that all the subtleties of disputations about person, Trinity, and the like be shaken out by the simpler folk, who if they cannot satisfy all rigid disputants, need not thereby distrust of their cause nor despair of the truth. For many things are true in themselves, though they cannot be immediately persuaded to all.

### Case IV. Whence can I be certain that the first person of the Trinity was Father from eternity

**Respondeo.** The Photinians disturb the consciences of men on this question, denying the eternal generation of the Son. They say we have no testimony of Scripture by which to prove the eternal paternity of God. But if God is not Father from eternity, then neither is the Son co-eternal — and consequently is not the true and natural God.

But it cannot be denied that in Scripture God is called the eternal Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, though *ad literam* nowhere so styled. For as to what some allege from Isa 9:6 — *the Father of eternity* — this makes nothing for the purpose of God the Father, nor of eternity *a priori* but *a futuro saeculo*, which the Son bore to us.

But it can be taught from other passages that God has been Father of his Only-begotten Son from eternity:

**1. From those Scriptures which speak of the eternal generation.** For if the Son is begotten from eternity, his Father also is Father from eternity. And this teaches David (Ps 2:7): *Thou art my Son; this day have I begotten thee* — which words are cited of Christ (Acts 13:33; Heb 1:5, 5:5). The *hodie* used of God does not note some definite time — for with God there is neither today nor yesterday, but *the perpetual NOW*. (In human things *hodie* sometimes means the same as *semper* — Matt 6:11: *Give us this day our daily bread*; Heb 3:7: *Today, if ye will hear his voice*; Heb 3:13: *exhort one another daily, so long as it is called Today*. But in divine things *hodie* notes perpetuity of the NOW.) Therefore of him who *today has been begotten by the Father*, it is said that blessed are they who trust in him — which is proper only to the true and eternal God. Therefore his generation also is eternal — for as the Son, so the generation; and as the Son, so the Father: *he is eternal, therefore this too*.

The same teaches Solomon (Prov 8:22 ff.), where the Son of God is thus introduced speaking: *The Lord possessed me in the beginning of his way, before his works of old.* In the Hebrew is *qanah* (possessed) — which word notes such signification as is by generation, as in Gen 4:1. So the Son says he was begotten by the Lord *from the beginning of his ways* — that is, *from eternity*, as in the same chapter (v. 23): *the heavenly Wisdom is called begotten*, when the deeps were not yet — where in Hebrew is *chollaltî*, which word is taken of the formation of the fetus in the womb (Ps 51:6); therefore the Lord was his Father from eternity. Hither pertains the saying of Micah (5:2), where of the ruler of Israel it is said: *whose goings forth have been from of old, from the days of eternity* — that is, *from eternity begotten by the Father*, for that going-forth which is by generation is here treated of, in which sense the little word *going-forth* is used in Scripture for *generation* (Gen 35:11; 46:26; Exod 1:5; Heb 7:5).

**2. From those Scriptures which speak of the eternal existence of the Son of God.** As John 1:1: *In the beginning was the Word* — that is, from eternity, for at the beginning of the creatures was mere eternity; and *the Word was in the beginning with the Father* (v. 2), therefore the Father also was in the beginning or from eternity. So to the Jews (John 8:58): *before Abraham was, I am.* Therefore he had a Father before Abraham, not according to the human nature (which was posterior to Abraham) — therefore according to the divine and eternal.

Others have used other places too: Calatinus (iv, c. 4) that Ps 72:17: *his name shall endure for ever, before the sun was his name.* Eusebius (i, c. 1) the saying of Ps 110:3: *before the day-star I begat thee.* Irenaeus (ii, c. 48) that saying of Isa 53:8: *who shall declare his generation?*

**3. From those Scriptures which treat of the eternal glory which he had always with the Father** — as when in John 17:5 he thus addresses the Father: *And now, O Father, glorify thou me with thine own self with the glory which I had with thee before the world was.* This glory the Father gave him (John 3:35). But no other medium of giving can be thought of than *generation*, through which the Father gave him all things (John 5:26). Since therefore before the constitution of the world all things were given him by the Father through generation — therefore from eternity also the Father was, and his generation eternal.

These things can satisfy the lover of truth, who by one operation from the sayings can demonstrate both Father and Son to be from eternity what they are — that is, Father and Son — according to the nature of correlates.

### Case V. How can a man persuade himself that in Christ are two natures

**Respondeo.** This question is not so much *theoretical* as *practical*. For the pious mind can doubt how two most diverse natures can come into one person, since they are endowed with plainly contrary properties — one mortal, the other immortal; one passible, the other impassible; one visible, the other invisible; one mutable, the other immutable. This reason moved the *Photinians* to hold Christ to be a mere man, as appears from the *Racovian Catechism* (fol. 48–49).

Here, that the pious conscience not be left in doubt, *first of all* it must be certain that the conjunction of two natures in Christ is *manifestly the great mystery of piety* (as Paul calls it, 1 Tim 3:16); therefore reason is not to be heard here, which does not understand the mysteries of faith. *When to thee, wondering, there falls a doubt: hurry to faith, which is the most ready solvent*, says Justin (d. 1).

Then, whereas in these mysteries useless is the question of *mode*, it suffices that from Scripture it is clear *of the very thing itself*. Whence Justin Martyr said: *from the same came that question of the Capernaites, in which they are gravely reproved* (John 6:41, 6:52). Even if therefore the mode of the union of two natures in one person of Christ cannot exactly be shown, it is enough for a faithful man that Scripture shows two natures in Christ, as we shall now hear.

Third — the two natures in Christ are indeed *diverse*, but not *contrary or contradictory* — for there is no contrariety of substances, as Aristotle says (*Categories* c. 5; *Elenchi* i, c. 5; *Metaphysics* iv, c. 6). It is required for opposition that it be made *according to the same, to the same, at the same time*. Neither are things opposed truly which are called *mortal and immortal, mutable and immutable*, since they are not said according to the same — but this of the human, that of the divine nature: which nonetheless afterwards is predicated of the whole supposit. So that Christ is called *mortal and immortal, but according to another nature* — of which the exemplum stands even in natural things: for body and soul are two most diverse natures — one visible, the other invisible; one irrational, the other rational — and yet in one man they come together. A similar thing can be said of iron and fire, which two most diverse natures with their most diverse qualities in ignited iron are united.

**With these removed which reason could object,** the pious mind before all things ought to consider that Jesus Christ came down from heaven for our salvation (which Scripture everywhere testifies — Matt 1:21; Luke 1:31, 9:56; Acts 4:12; 1 Tim 1:15). Christ our ratio required to be *God and Man*. For God was offended by the sins of men, who could not be appeased except by God. *Man cannot give God his ransom, the price of the redemption of his soul* (Ps 49:8). Now appeasement consists in remission of sins, which *without shedding of blood cannot happen* (Heb 9:22). Hence Ambrose (on Luke 4): *No one of men could be such that he might take away the sins of the whole world; and yet who was so great a man, in whom the sins of all men should be washed away?*

It behooved therefore our Saviour also to be partaker of flesh and blood, and thus to be God and Man — for which cause Paul (Acts 20:28) conjoins each nature in the work of our salvation. Whence Anselm (*Cur Deus homo* i, c. 6): *the satisfaction cannot be made except by God, nor ought it, except by man; therefore, existing through the human, he pays what is owed, and through the divine can, what is expedient.*

Then let the pious mind consider how often *Christ himself*, of whose human nature no one doubted, called himself *God* — and indeed such a God as *works in the same manner* (as appears from John 5:17), whence he is accused by the Jews of *making himself equal with God* (v. 18). Nor did he say this merely, but by *divine works* — namely searching hearts, miraculous healing of the sick, resurrection of the dead, etc. — he demonstrated himself to be such. Therefore rightly is faith to be given him. For the examples show that always it went ill with those who arrogated to themselves divine honors not due — Adam (Gen 3), Herod (Acts 12:23), Simon Magus (Irenaeus i, c. 20; Eusebius ii, c. 1).

[*Of this Simon Magus can be read also Egesippus (iii, c. 2), who refers how Simon Magus wished, mediating through his own magical art, to ascend into heaven — but at the prayers of the Apostle Peter fell and was broken about the bones of his other foot.*]

To these, moreover, are added the testimonies of Scripture concerning the incarnation of the Son of God, manifestly speaking: *the Word became flesh* (John 1:14); *the fullness of the Godhead dwells bodily in Christ* (Col 2:9); *God was manifested in the flesh* (1 Tim 3:16); *he took part of flesh and blood, as the children* (Heb 2:14); *he took not on him the nature of angels, but the seed of Abraham* (v. 16). Also those places where express mention is made of the two natures — that Christ is said to be *of the seed of David according to the flesh* (Rom 1:3); that he *suffered in the flesh* (1 Pet 4:1) — which conjoining would have not been necessary, if besides the human, no other nature had been in him.

At length, as it were from abundance, with these testimonies of Scripture can be joined *the pagans' prodigies and oracles* which speak of the divinity of Christ and his admirable incarnation. So (as Suidas relates) the Emperor Augustus, consulting Delphi shortly before Christ's nativity as to who after him would rule in the world, received this reply: *A Hebrew boy commands the blessed gods; he bids leave this dome, and to return to hell — go forth silent from our altars.* Whereupon, being moved, Augustus reared an altar on the Capitol with this inscription: *HAEC EST ARA PRIMOGENITI DEI* (This is the altar of the Firstborn of God) — see Suetonius and Cornelius Tacitus. Plutarch reports it thus:

> *Me a Hebrew boy, God himself ruling,*
> *Bids me leave the seat, and sad return under Orcus:*
> *An altar therefore hence in silence recede from our.*

And *at the same time through an entire day a precious oil issued from the earth* (Eutropius).
