Such is [attributed to] the Sibylla Erithrea: *In the newest times God shall be humbled, and shall be humbled by divine offspring, and shall lie down in a stable, and by the office of a maiden shall be reared God and Man.* Item, what Boethius writes (*Discipl. schol.*): *in the tomb of Plato a leaf was found, in which was written: I believe in the Son of God, who shall be born of a Virgin, who shall suffer for the human race, and on the third day shall rise.* Similar things Nicephorus reports (xii, c. 34) of the time of Constantine sixth, and his mother Irene — that a stone tomb was found under the earth, with this inscription. See more of this kind in *Johannes Nauclerus* (first century). See earlier verses of the Greeks cited from Lactantius.

In all these, but especially those which are drawn from Scripture, the conscience of a pious man can and ought to rest — for the evasions of unbelievers are not so great as to be able to overturn this faith, since there is nothing so solid in Scripture that the impious would not seek to evade, though with vain effort.

### Case VI. Whether God and man in Christ are so one, as the three persons in the Trinity are one God

**Respondeo.** The fathers were wont to compare the union of two natures in Christ with the unity of the three persons in God — not as if there were one and the same ratio of union in both, but rather because there is between these two a certain reciprocal collation.

**The union of natures in Christ is not in all things convertible with the unity of persons in God, but by way of contrariety.** So *as in the Trinity nature and essence are one, but the three persons are distinct — so on the contrary in Christ, there is one person, but the natures and essences are two.*

**Second — in the Deity, diverse are the ratios of distinction and communication.** For communication in divinity looks to nature, but distinction to persons: on the contrary, in Christ distinction looks to the two natures, but communication to the person. So it is that the *idioms of the divine nature*, not naturally or essentially but personally, are believed to be communicated to the human nature. For *in the Trinity of persons is a natural essential union, but in Christ the union of two natures is not essential, but personal*, since both together constitute the person.

Damascene indeed calls this union in Christ *substantial* (*hypostatic*) (*De fide orth.* iii, c. 3) — but in no other sense than that he might oppose it to *verbal* union, which is empty and *unsubstantial* (κατ᾽ ἐπίνοιαν, purely notional) — and *essential* to him is the same as *real*, for he says: *we call this union essential, that is, real, κατ᾽ ἀλήθειαν, not κατ᾽ ἐπίνοιαν made.*

### Case VII. Can we rightly think of the two natures in Christ as of two parts of a composite

**Respondeo.** Orthodox theologians have not shunned to call the human nature part of the person of Christ — for by which Christ is called, since it is not his accident but a part which pertains to the integrity of this composite person; without which Christ would not be Christ or anointed. And they also call the person of Christ *composite* — but they do not understand any gross and physical composition, but a person incarnate consisting of two integral and perfect natures.

The scholastics say that this composition of person is not made *by reason of parts*, but *by reason of one* — since not every one, in which two things come together, can be called composed of them.

Rightly and piously therefore is Christ now, after the incarnation, thought of as of a whole *composed of his natures as of parts*. For even if the *Logos* was already before the incarnation a perfect person, yet simple; now after the incarnation a *composite* person, which is called Christ, has been made — for the assumed nature to that same second person of the Trinity pertains, not aside, separately, and posed to the part, but in the tightest and most present way is united to the *Deity of the Logos*.

Whence the ancients did not doubt to say of the assumed nature that it was received *into the consort of the Holy Trinity* — because the second person of the Trinity consists of two natures, human and divine, united to each other most tightly, so that one from another cannot even *notionally* (as Damascene speaks) be torn — therefore *of the natures in Christ we can indeed think as of the parts of a composite whole; but not as of separated parts, but as being in one whole most united.*

### Case VIII. Whether one may say without sin, *the flesh of Christ became God*, as it is said *the Word became flesh*

**Respondeo.** In locutions about the union, this assertion is dangerous — for since not before was the flesh, than that it was assumed, by this phrase false occasion would be given to opinion, as if the flesh before it was united to the *Logos* pre-existed in its own proper hypostasis, and by this pre-existence would anticipate the union, and afterwards indeed was made God — which is false and erroneous. Rather then it should be said: *Christ according to the flesh is God* — or, as Scripture speaks, *of the seed of David is Jehovah* (Jer 23:6), and *the second man is himself the Lord from heaven* (1 Cor 15:47). For as according to Deity by nature he is God, so according to humanity he attained by grace that name which is above every name.

Meanwhile, however, to express that *admirable communication of natures* in Christ, some eminent fathers did not hesitate to use also the proposition *reciprocal* — among whom is Athanasius, who of the human nature writes against Apollinaris: *God himself became flesh, that flesh itself might be made God the Word.* And a little after: *So it stands that in the flesh the Son and the Father's Word was seen; and again that same which was seen was called the Word of life* by sacred letter. So likewise Damascene (Or. 1 *De imag.*): *Just as the Word without change became flesh, remaining what he was, so the flesh was made the Word, not losing that which it is, but rather with the Word made the same, so far as pertains to the person.*

These of course have been written in a sound and orthodox sense by the Fathers — whom, however, some of the more recent (especially *Jesuits*) fail to hold, willing to teach nothing else than what they otherwise teach: the hypostasis of the Word to be that of the flesh itself, and the hypostasis of the flesh to be that of the divinity — which from his hypostasis do not really differ, but he is participant of it, just as Nazianzen taught the flesh to be received into consort of the Deity; Eusebius likewise established that the Word deigned to assume a man to the dignity and beatitude of divinity — nor did Damascene otherwise mean, teaching the flesh of the incarnate Son of God to be made participant of the divine nature, since it belongs to it not by nature but by grace and personal union.

Though we theologians may use this locution — this and no other sense which is sufficiently orthodox — because however such locutions can be depraved by calumny from ill-intent, and can be otherwise queried, and because with the very first *Servetus* used this to establish his error (by which he wished to remove the human nature of Christ from the number of creatures), it is safer indeed to abstain from unstable phrases, and not to draw them into disputation, save as far as they are surveyed among the Fathers — so that appears that we bring their locutions rather than our own, and this only that they be not left as an occasion of pleasure, and yet not so obscured, that these ecclesiastical writers may be held suspected of the real communion of natures — that to express that same *nothing sperm-sinning* which is between them for pronouncing; the flesh be said to be made Word, and the divinity to be flesh-made.

### Case IX. Whence I am certain and can persuade myself that the Holy Spirit is a Deity truly and a natural God, subsisting person by himself, personally distinct from the Father and the Son

**Respondeo.** This part of our faith the Photinians render doubtful, who indeed concede the Holy Spirit to be something certain and divine (for they call him the *power* of God), but deny that he is a God and Person of the Deity subsisting per se — but they in themselves the Holy Spirit call now the *Gospel*, now the *hope of the promise* — all which things are exploded and refuted in the refutation of the *Racovian Catechism*.

**Of two things therefore must the pious man be persuaded: that the Holy Spirit is *true God*, and that he is a *person by himself* subsistent of the Deity, distinct from the Father and the Son.**

**Foundations of the prior assertion** (that the Holy Spirit is true God):

**1. Because in Scripture the Holy Spirit is expressly called God**, whether the name of God be proper to the Father or to the Son. So 1 Sam 23:2 (Vulg. 23:1): *The Spirit of the Lord spake within me: God of Israel spake, saying, He that ruleth over men must be just*. Here the Holy Spirit is called *God of Israel*. So Num 12:6 *Jehovah says, I have spoken through the mouth of the holy Prophets*, and Peter says that *God has spoken through the mouth of his holy Prophets* — this said the Holy Spirit, as is written in Heb 3:7. And what Isa 6:9 the Prophet heard, to the Holy Spirit it is referred (Acts 28:25). Eph 4:30: *the Holy Spirit says: harden not your hearts today*, which the Holy Spirit has said, is written of. And *Ananias* is said to have lied to the Holy Spirit (Acts 5:3), and a little further it is said to Peter *he lied not to men but to God* (v. 4).

**2. Because to the Holy Spirit are attributed *proper properties of the Deity*.** As *eternity* — the Holy Spirit is *not among created things*, because he is *incorruptible before creatures* (Gen 1:2). To him is attributed *immensity* (Ps 139:7); *omnipresence* (Wisd 12:1); to him is attributed *knowledge of all things* (Jer 40:13; John 14:26) — whence he is called *the Spirit of wisdom, understanding, counsel* (Isa 11:2); *he searches all things, even the deep things of God* (1 Cor 2:10); *he is attributed omnipotence* (Wisd 12:18; Luke 15:10; Acts 10:38); *of the Spirit of strength and roboris he is called* (Isa 11:11; 2 Tim 1:7), *who works all in all* (Wisd 7:22; 1 Cor 12:6).

**3. Scripture ascribes to the Holy Spirit *divine works*.** As *creation* — *the Spirit of God moved upon the face of the waters* (Gen 1:2); *he made me, and the Spirit of the Almighty gave me life* (Job 33:4). *Preservation* — he governs and preserves the Church through the ministry of the Word (1 Cor 3:16; John 3:5–8; 1 Cor 12:11).

**4. Scripture assigns him *divine honor*, because he is adored by angels** (Isa 6:8–9 — which place is expressly to the Holy Spirit referred, Acts 28:25); *in his name we are baptized* (Matt 28:19) — which mode belongs only to God's name to be attempted (Ps 76:12). More testimonies of this kind can be gathered from Scripture, on which see Chemnitz in *Loci*. Concerning Titian — his evasions can indeed be circumvented, but never can they overturn: for such evasions can be seen refuted elsewhere.

**Then, that the Holy Spirit is a *person* of the Trinity distinct from the Father and Son by real distinction**, appears from these foundations:

**1. Because Scripture attributes to him understanding and will**, from which two the truth of a person is collected — *understanding* indeed, when it calls him *Spirit of wisdom, understanding, counsel* (Isa 11:2), *Spirit of revelation* (Eph 1:17), *who searches all things, even the deep things of God* (1 Cor 2:10); *will* when the Spirit is said to *breathe where he wills* (John 3:8; 1 Cor 12:11).

**2. Because to the Holy Spirit are attributed *actions* distinct from the Father and Son.** But *actions properly are of singulars* — as singulars are, so also the acting things are. So the *Holy Spirit is said to convince the world of sin* (John 16:7); *to intercede* (Rom 8:26); *to make aware of the truth* (John 14:26; 15:26); *to send prophets* (Acts 13:2); *to bring gifts* (1 Cor 12:11); *to bear witness with our spirit* (Rom 8:16). *Actions* also attributed to inanimate things (as when charity is said to lead, sin to seduce, writing to teach, the law to convict the world, the Gospel to console) are *metonymic* — where by adjunction of subjects the actions of accidents are ascribed to substances. But to the Holy Spirit, actions are properly assigned — from which appears that the Holy Spirit is also said to work through doctrine, through charity, wisdom, power; and through the Word or Scripture speaks and testifies, through the Law convicts the world, through the Gospel consoles: nowhere are charity, Scripture, the Law and the like said to act *through* anything else.

**3. The Holy Spirit is distinguished by a *peculiar action* from the Father and the Son in the baptism of Christ, when he descended in the *species* of a dove, and was seen over Christ (Matt 3:16); in the outpouring of the Holy Spirit upon the Apostles, where the Son sends the Holy Spirit from the Father (John 15:26). The Holy Spirit appeared in the species of tongues of fire seated upon each of the Apostles. In *species* he is distinguished from the Father and Son, as we now hear (John 14:16 f., 15:26); *he proceeds from the Father* (John 15:26); *he proceeds from both* (John 16:15).

*Objection*: From these foundations Photinians can respond, that the Holy Spirit is a certain *power of God sanctifying*, as Ostorodius speaks (*Instit.* c. 4, s. 5); and so is not some person of the Deity a second — which is unique principle — but an attribute in God, as is love, mercy, omnipotence, etc.

But it stands against: (1) that in Isa 63:10 he is said to *be irritated, to punish, to be angry* — none of which can be said of an attribute; (2) that in the same place he is distinguished from the Father — for he is called *attribute*, and indeed by a peculiar action distinguished, since to be irritated to anger cannot be said of mercy, omnipotence, and the like; (3) that he is said to be sent by the Father, to proceed from the Father — which of the power of God is not said, for these are notes of persons and are denied of the Father; (4) it is called *virtue of another one*, and *virtue of Christ* — but the *virtue of the Highest* (Luke 1:35), the *virtue of the Holy Spirit* (Luke 24:49); *by the virtue of the Holy Spirit alone* (Rom 15:19). So simply, is not the same as the Father, that which is called *other from the Father* (John 14:16 f.); the virtue of any is not something else from him, but a certain something *with* him.

Then it must be known that the *virtue of God* is taken in Scripture in three ways: (1) *effectively* — noting a certain divine force in the good, and God's gifts in men — this is what is called *the virtue of the Holy Spirit* (Luke 1:8); (2) *essentially*, for the infinite power in God, as 2 Cor 12:9: *my virtue is made perfect in weakness*; (3) *hypostatically* of the third person of the Trinity, who is the Holy Spirit — for so it is called *the virtue of the Almighty*, *the virtue of the Highest*, as from the collation of the places Luke 24:49 and Acts 1:4–5 is gathered. The Photinians therefore confuse the third signification with the second — since they wish to remove the peculiar person of the Holy Spirit from the third of the Trinity, whom they falsely conflate with the second, which is uniquely principle. But the Holy Spirit is the person by himself subsistent — as by such testimonies as we have hitherto adduced, which for the conscience of the pious man can abundantly satisfy.

— ❦ —

## Chapter III. On the Attributes of God

*De attributis divinis.*

Of the essence and persons divine, thus far: there follow the attributes of God, whose consideration has great force in the souls of the pious for exciting piety and preserving religion. And though of these many things are disputed theoretically, in practice too some cases occur, of which now in order we shall see.

### Case I. Since God is most wise, omnipotent, good, just, holy — it seems that God is to be conceived as a subject with his accidents, which imports a composition, contrary to divine simplicity

**Respondeo.** Far be it, that in God we posit any composition properly so called. For therefore *there is no variableness with him, neither shadow of turning* (James 1:17), he is *always the same* (Ps 90:2), and he *remains eternally* (Ps 110:4) — for eternity always excludes the mutability of accidents.

Then Scripture speaks accurately of God: for God is called *charity itself* (1 John 4:7, 16), because *charity, mercy, justice, wisdom* itself belong to him (Sir 1:2; 24:9); he is called *strength* itself (1 John 1:5), *God is light itself* (v. 6); and this is not said by any figure of God, as when perhaps some man is called *wisdom itself* on account of the excellent wisdom in him — but properly, because mercy, justice, wisdom, and the rest which we say of God, are *God himself*. So Bernard writes: *these five names of God — God for us — that we may bear in our heart mercy, wisdom, justice, strength, power, greatness of God: as it were the five names of God are called.*

**Power, goodness, wisdom, in God are called *attributes*, not qualities or accidents, since they do not really differ from the essence.** This doctrine has its foundation in Scripture, on which conscience can safely rest.

But two things here are to be observed: **(i)** what is denied is *proper accidents in God* — that is, real accidents. But *predicated accidents* (i.e., *accidental predicates*) can be found in God, as when God's willing to create the world *is an accident*. For he could have not created it. So the actions of God about external objects can be predicated of him *accidentally*, though they do not inhere in him accidentally. Whence the Scholastics rightly note: *the actions of God, so far as they proceed from God, are not accidental to God — but as they are terminated in creatures, or as they signify a certain habitude to creatures, they are accidental to him.* Example: *creation of the world is an accident, for it could have not happened*. God created the world, which nevertheless in the most simple essence of God imports no mutation or composition, because it is neither a part of the essence, nor a quality, nor a property, but only a determination of the divine will to external objects, which *saving the immutability of the essence of God* varies in mode. Similarly, *to be adored, to be worshipped, to be loved by the faithful* — is an *accidens praedicabile*, but not *accidens praedicamentale* in God: for the potency of creating is essential to God.

**(ii)** God's *attributes* are with the essence *the same thing*, and consequently with the essence not distinguished in reality: but *the same are among themselves*, so that God's goodness is his power, God's justice is his mercy — for they do not have the same definition. Therefore our intellect, though in God they are conjoined, distinctly conceives, and of each separately and distinctly judges — since through the weakness of our nature otherwise it cannot. So let none suppose, when he distinctly thinks about God's goodness, wisdom, power, that he thereby destroys divine simplicity by imagining an *ens complex* — but he should imagine one, which is the *one thing itself simply*, all attributes joined together in the essence.

### Case II. Since Scripture often attributes to God mercy, wrath, hatred, love, penitence — how are we rightly to conceive of all these?

**Respondeo.** *First of all it is to be known: to attribute to God wrath and mercy is not metaphorical but proper — true, not simulated.* For God is truly angered by the sins of men — not, as men are, *by tumult of the soul*, but by pure judgment against the sinner, as against him *there is wrath upon such a one* (Isa 64:5). *He that believeth not the Son* shall not see life, but the wrath of God abideth upon him (John 3:36); *the wrath of God is revealed from heaven upon all iniquity of men* (Rom 1:18). When one is *definite* in wrath, then it is said to *have mercy* — *not eternally will I contend against man, nor to end will I be angry*, says the Lord (Isa 57:16), and to *Simon Peter* he says: *not until seven times, but seventy times seven* — so is God, as also in the Lord's Prayer we ask: *forgive us our sins, as we forgive those who sin against us*. Elsewhere is it said: *God is mercy* (Sir 2). And Christ is called *mercy* (Luke 1:78) — that is, our *very mercy*. Similarly of goodness — the Psalmist says *thou art good, and thou dost good* (Ps 119:68), and elsewhere calls God himself *good*.

To Solomon indeed (1 Kgs 3:14, 18) the Lord says of not remembering iniquity of sinners — whence is understood that goodness in God is not an accident, but is God himself. And of the Father: *am I not merciful to thee, as I ought* — Genesis (49:6); Jer 49:11; Hos 6:9; Zech 1:16 — *he swears by himself.*

Two things however here must be observed: **(i)** what we deny is that accidents fall upon God — namely *accidents of an inhering figure*, not *accidents of a predicable figure*. Many things are predicated of God accidentally which yet inhere not in him accidentally. Whence the Scholastics teach: *there are three modes by which God is said to be different, or is compared to accidents by way of example. Grace, in the creation of the world, is an accident: for it could have not been created. But he created, of which if he had done it, in his most simple essence he would have imported no mutation or composition — because they are neither parts of the essence, nor qualities, nor properties, but only determinations of the divine will to external objects, which, saving the immutable essence of God, vary in mode.*

**(ii)** It must be observed further, that though the *attributes* of God are with his essence *one thing*, and consequently not really distinct from the essence — yet *among themselves they are not entirely one and the same*, so that God's goodness would be his power, or God's justice his mercy: for they do not have the same ratio of definition. Therefore our intellect, though in God they are conjoined, distinctly conceives, and of each separately and distinctly judges — since through the weakness of our nature otherwise it cannot. Whence let no one, when he thinks distinctly about God's goodness, wisdom, or power, suppose that thereby he descends from the simplicity of God's essence, and imagines an *ens complexum*; but let him think that God is the *thing one and simple*, in whom whatsoever he can think several of are all in truth the same simplicity of essence.

*Sometimes*, moreover, the change of will is attributed to God — because as the deed changes, so also God says now the opposite; as when God repents of his mercy or of his wrath, no real change is in him but in men — as the sun perpetually is the same, and does not itself experience mutation, but men are changed by the heat and light of the sun. Concerning this Augustine wrote (*De civitate Dei* xii, c. 17): *God is said to change his will, when to men who unchangingly foreknew that they would change, he does now this to make him — thus indeed to be changed as *in a certain mode-changing way* they find him.*

In man, penitence arises partly from ignorance and error, partly from mutability of affections: in God it happens in neither mode, for neither is there in him lack of counsel or error, nor is there weakness of affections. Of the whole general kind we treat in Wisd 11:27: *God is not moved with human suffering, but is judgment which is imposed as penalty for sin. The cogitation of God is a recognition of things to be done*, being immutable.

In man true is the mutation, when either he becomes merciful or when he repents of some thing; but when *God is said to repent*, no true mutation happens in God, but in man — as also when God is said to have hands, feet, viscera, this is a *metaphorical* locution: for as God has no viscera, so also *such motion cannot be attributed to him*. In man is a true mutation, since he becomes merciful, or since he repents of something; but when *God is said to be angry, to punish, to be irritated*, no mutation happens in God, but in man — just as the sun is perpetually the same, nor does experience mutation, but men are changed by the heat and light of the sun.

### Case III. Whether one may say without sin that something is impossible to God — for it seems to be blasphemy

**Respondeo.** This assertion seems indeed to be *directly* against the words of the angel: *no word shall be impossible with God* (Luke 1:37); as also elsewhere it is said: *nothing is impossible for God to be moved* (Heb 6:18); *God cannot deny himself* (2 Tim 2:13). So also it is said: God cannot suffer, cannot receive anything from elsewhere — and yet God is called *omnipotent* (in Symbol), *seeing that whatever pleases him, he does* (Ps 135:6). And Paul speaks (Acts 3:25; Prov 10:16).

We must know therefore that *one* is *active potency*, *another passive potency*: the former is in God, the latter no way — for the latter includes vice, defect, and imperfection, which in creatures is found, but in God is not — as *to be able to lie or sin, to die, to suffer, to be capable of pain, to eat, to drink, to sleep*, since these too are of imperfection, nor are they without passive potency. Concerning all these it is truly said that God *cannot do them, because he is omnipotent* — as Augustine (*De symbolo* i, c. 1): *God is therefore omnipotent, because he cannot do these things: for if he could, he would not be omnipotent. As the very potency of God is, so is he — not to be able to lie is a virtue.*

For which reason all things which involve contradiction — as it were are subject to the divine power, and which imperfection includes and destroys, are contrary to the nature of God — hence commonly it is said, *God cannot contradict himself, and thus it is not so that in God all things are impossible potentially*.

But this rule must be held: *contradictions of reason are not to be opposed to the Word of God* — else all the mysteries of the faith perish. Contradictions were seen to Sarah both to hear and to bear — that from a decrepit body a son should be born (Gen 18:14); yet Abraham had heard the promise: *thy old age is not fainter than her own, but he was fully persuaded that what God had promised he was able to perform* (Rom 4:21). It is contradictory to reason that a body be and *be in several places*, because reason thinks that any natural body is written in only one place at once. Whence Peter Martyr writes (in Judges c. 6): *there are three impossibilities to be affirmed of the created thing*, and Keckerman (*System of Theology* c. 1) reckons among the impossibilities to God the omnipresence of Christ's flesh, the flesh's ubiquity, and oral manducation of Christ's flesh.

But these contradictions are not to be *falsely opposed to the Word*, in which whatsoever God promised, he is also able to perform, though to us it may seem impossible. For what *is impossible with men, is possible with God* (Luke 18:27); God can *super-abundantly do above all that we ask or think* (Eph 3:20).

### Case IV. Wherein is to be understood that something proceeds from the wisdom of God as infinite

**Respondeo.** Not because it can produce an infinite effect: this involves contradiction, and is contrary to God's nature. Nor is any creature properly capable of infinity; but only God is *ens infinitum*, who if he produced another infinite, would produce nothing but God himself — which is contradictory.

But infinite may be said of the *potency of God*:
- **(i)** *In and by himself*, or by his own nature: since it is God's essential idiom, that he is called infinite.
- **(ii)** *In respect of the objects*, which are not merely those already made — for varied and even infinite can still be made by him if his wisdom pleases.
- **(iii)** *In respect of the mode of operating*, since God never operates so intensely and efficaciously, that he cannot operate more intensely and more efficaciously.
- **(iv)** *In respect of the matter* in which it acts, which to God can never be lacking, since prepared matter he needs not — and if he wills, in a light action he can prepare it.

### Case V. How can we perfectly conceive and rightly speak about the *omnipresence* of God

**Respondeo.** Many things about this are today disputed; but the conscience of the pious can rest in this general matter by the following observations:

**1.** Scripture attributes to God *essential presence with creatures*, which others call *coëxistentia* or *indistantia* — by which word Scripture denotes God to be present to all creatures, though he can be separated from them. So Isa 66:1: *God is present to creatures without expansion*. Whence the ancients said: *God is above all, not by height; below all, not by lowness; before all, not by antecedence; behind all, not by consequence; among all, not by inclusion; outside all, not by exclusion. God is above all, presiding; below all, sustaining; before all, foreseeing; behind all, remembering; among all, filling all; outside all, containing all.* Item: *God is outside all created things, but not excluded; within all, but not enclosed; below all, but not depressed; above all, but not exalted; before all, not preceding; behind all, not following.*

**2.** He is present to creatures without expansion, without diffusion or essence-multiplication, but by simple essential infinity. *He remains within all, himself outside all; himself above all, himself below all; himself internally by presence, himself externally by potency.*

**3.** He is present without inclusion — for *the earth is a mustard-seed, contained within it Job holds*, that is, *within it all things are, rather than that itself is at anything, yet no way in nothing, so that he himself is contained; for man is in space, but God is not, since with his length, breadth, and height everything is one and everywhere he is; not in things, but in things above all is himself* (Anselm).

**4.** He is present without emanation or evacuation of himself — as neither by emissions of any power of his own, does he flow from him and fill the universe, since no creature is outside God, who wholly is in himself everywhere.

**5.** He is present, not indeed in *substance* in heaven and on earth, but by *power and force*, as the Osianiani think — but by his essence he fills heaven and earth, which by *virtue and power* is distinct indeed, but not separate.

[*See on God p. 19 (Anlin. Reg. 9). Damascus: God is a circle whose center is everywhere, circumference nowhere. Calvinism in Jer 23: "God everywhere fills himself in every place." The ethnics said: *All things are full of Jove*. Multa in the same on God is God (Chrysostom).*]

**6.** He is present *sound and secure* — not that one part is in one place, another in another; he is not distributed, but *in the whole is whole, and equally in every part is whole* — since we cannot say exactly how God is by his essence everywhere. Here all things are to be removed which import imperfection, so as to belong to God (Chrysostom Hom. 2 on Hebrews): *God is understood by intelligence beyond understanding.*

**7.** The purity of the divine essence is not polluted by any creatures with which he is present — as also the soul of man is even in whatever part of the vilest body, nor is it soiled, and the sun's rays penetrate filthy places without their pollution (Athanasius, *De incarnatione Christi* c. 12).

**8.** The presence of God with creatures has four grades:
- **(i)** *General* — of the creation and government: whence *God fills heaven and earth* (Jer 23:24), and *he is not far from any one of us* (Acts 17:27).
- **(ii)** *Gracious* — when God fills the hearts of the faithful with his grace, whom he leads, teaches, defends: whence *the hearts of the faithful with the Holy Spirit are filled with the Lord's presence* (1 Cor 6:19). Of this presence the Son of God says: *I will come to him, and make my abode with him* (John 14:23); *I am with you always, even unto the end of the world* (Matt 28:20).
- **(iii)** *Glorious* — when God is present to the elect in eternal life, whom he fully illumines and suffuses with eternal joy: of which Christ says: *where I am, there also shall my minister be* (John 12:26); *we shall always be with the Lord* (1 Thess 4:17).
- **(iv)** *Singular* — the inhabitation of the *Logos* in the assumed flesh, of which Paul treats (Col 2:9). In all these grades, with the presence of God is conjoined his operation, by which the grades are distinguished among themselves — for what is present, is common to all grades: but the *mode of presence* is taken from the diversity of the operations. For otherwise God operates in creatures with which by general presence he is present, namely by sustaining and preserving them (*in him we live, and move, and have our being* — Acts 17:28); otherwise in the faithful, to whom he is present with grace, namely by inhabiting, consoling, defending them, whose benefits he simultaneously promises whenever he promises presence to the faithful; otherwise he operates in his elect and blessed inhabitants of heaven, to whom he makes himself for seeing, and for immersing them in the immensity of pure Deity, so that he acts through them as through his proper flock.

*From this it is clear that we do not, without distinct reason, need to separate omnipresence, which by its own name involves operation, from absolute omnipresence.* For the very word alone denotes presence of essence with creatures, but the operation is included: since Scripture describes God's omnipresence not by mere *absolute presence* but by the manner in which he is present through his operation in each grade — so that the mode is like a form or specific difference, which does not enter into the definition itself.

### Case VI. What of those who suffer the iniquity of others

*Since it happens often that some suffer the iniquity of others (Exod 34:7; Prov 7:24), where it seems that God visits the sin of the parent on the innocent children — this is greatly a scruple for the pious, that God is unjust.*

**Respondeo.** Though the justice of God no man ought to call in doubt (for *just are his judgments, uprightly all his ways*, and *the whole earth is filled with them* — Ps 145:17), yet many trouble even the pious to these thoughts, as the examples of Job, David, Jeremiah, Habakkuk testify — who on this account even expostulated with God (Job 21:7 ff.; Ps 73:3 ff.; Jer 12:1; Hab 1:2 ff.). Consequently for the pious mind: *nothing to God is unjust*.

But that nothing may fall against conscience, first of all be it considered that no one in the world is so holy but that he is conceived and born in sins — and often sins by his own good works. *Who dare say his heart is clean and he is free from sin?* (Prov 20:9). *There is not a just man upon earth that doeth good and sinneth not* (Eccl 7:20; 1 Kgs 8:46; Sir 7:9). *Behold, I was shapen in iniquity; and in sin did my mother conceive me* (Ps 51:5). *There is none innocent in the sight of God* (Job 15:14). Therefore if God is the avenger of crimes, he does no man injury, but rather bestows on all his mercy.

Then judgment must be made of the *calamities of the pious and the felicity of the impious*. The pious surely have from this life the promise (*godliness has promise of the life that now is and of that which is to come* — 1 Tim 4:8), yet always with the exception of the *cross*: for *all who will live godly in Christ Jesus shall suffer persecution* (2 Tim 3:12). The calamity of the pious is not counted as *poena* (this belongs to the enemies of God, not to the reconciled) but as *cross* — by which God exercises the patience of his own, preserves them from sins, separates them from the world, and prepares them for eternal joys. Whence *neither does this pain endure long, nor do the fortunes of the pious ever leave to be miserable* — but by God's felicity they are compensated.

*Four causes of the afflictions of the good can be given*: (i) the eternal decree of God, made for his own glory; (ii) the sins of holy men, on account of which they are punished — e.g., Adam, who by his lapse was punished with his posterity; (iii) *cross* alone, as a testimony of confession (of idolators and of superstition and of unlawful things, etc.); (iv) as *paideia* — the discipline of parents, as in Joseph's example (imprisonment).

Contrariwise, the felicity of the impious is not true, but as if of a shadow — partly because it consists only of the sweetness of the palate, as much as of pleasantness; partly of the very few because they *quickly pass away* (as in Ps 73:19 f.); and finally by *eternal torments* is compensated. Their path is unto ruin. So the sacred verse:

> *God's felicity treasures up for the wicked wrath in the day of wrath* (Rom 2:5).

*It is just then before God to render vengeance to those who trouble you, and to give to us who are troubled, rest with you, when the Lord Jesus shall be revealed from heaven* (2 Thess 1:6–7). Wonderfully however does God exercise this justice of his: sometimes in this life, while he afflicts his own — he does them good; for from this reason he *first sets on the palm of the impious felicity*, so that afterwards he might humble and reject them. And when he indulges pleasures to the impious, by that very fact he punishes them — for the hearts of the impious are depressed, they cleave to earthly things and cannot cleave to God, than whom nothing is more sweet to the pious. So *the flowers of the impious*, as *a beast of pasture, in the day of penalty are gathered* — that they may be matter for divine glory, as of Pharaoh it is said: *I have raised thee up*, that in thee I might show my power (Exod 9:16), *that thy furor be made a subject of celebration for me* (Ps 76:10). This is deferred into another world: they will differ, that they may bear judgment more grievously, *or, as Rachel says: they will be gathered in the day of judgment*, and the *life of the impious will be reversed by everlasting cross*.

Therefore ours is not to precipitate judgment about this, but by David's example (Ps 73:16) to conclude:

> *He is blessed, whom I love in God, who in the midst of the calamities of this life is tranquil of soul, as I have said,*
> *while the pit is dug for the impious.*

Then, that *in particular* judgment about the children of the evil parents pertains — is to be considered. Sirach 41:8: *Wretched are their children, because they know not the fear of the Lord.* But this is understood of the *original sin* and of the *actual* — for actual sin from parents is derived into children, of whom it becomes as it were their proper hereditary; and so if posterity is now punished on account of the fall of first parents, it is not indeed on account of the *foreign*, but the *proper* — for by parents in themselves the sin was purged, they are punished: as elsewhere Scripture says: *the fathers eat sour grapes, and the children's teeth are set on edge* (Jer 31:29 f.) — which Sirach 25:24 confirms in the psalm.

But by *some special grace*, God transfers the punishment of parents *unto the third and fourth generation of those that hate him*, and *shows mercy unto thousands to those that love him and keep his commandments* — where about the good and bad part of children is treated, who turned to the ways of the Lord act justly (as v. 19 expounds). But if into the malice of parents children have consented, then justly they are punished — not so much on account of *foreign*, as on account of *proper* offense, according to the most grave sentence of God (Exod 20:5): *I am the Lord thy God, a strong and jealous God, visiting the iniquity of the fathers upon the children unto the third and fourth generation of those that hate me* (cf. Deut 5:9).

But what if *the children themselves*, on account of the offenses of their evil parents, into which they *never consented*, are involved in evil with them together (as with infants in the flood) — nonetheless God does no injury to them, for even in infants he finds sin worthy of death; and to show his wrath against sin, he also involves the children who before the world are innocent in calamities of parents *even to a fourth generation*. But only in *temporal* calamities, which nothing can derogate from *their* felicity — for eternal penalty no one suffers except for his own delict, as is *established*.

— ❦ —

## Chapter IV. On Cases of Conscience Concerning the External Works of God

*De casibus conscientiae circa opera Dei externa.*

The works of God are either *within his very essence*, or *outside* — the internal works are also called *ad-intra proceedings*. Hence the other division is into *internal* and *external* works. *Internal works* distinguish the persons of the Holy Trinity, and are called by the Scholastics *personal* — of the Father, *to beget the Son*; of the Son, *to be begotten*; of the Holy Spirit, *to proceed from the Father and the Son*. These personal properties distinguish the persons from one another really. *External works* are common to all three persons and consist in the *creation, sustenance, government* of things; in *predestination, redemption*, and *sanctification* of men, and things similar. Hence the rule: *the works of the Trinity outward are undivided.*

Internal works we leave to the schools of controversy; concerning external works there are certain practical questions, of which conscience seeks to be established, which we shall see in order.

### Case I. Is it sufficiently safe to ask what God did before he created heaven and earth

Socrates (*Historia Tripartita* i, on the works of the Devil) reports a certain frivolous man who, in questions of this kind, was told that God had built hell, in which such curious minds might pay the penalty for their curiosity. And Basil is our own — that in the creation of the world we ought to coerce our thoughts, and by humble spirit not seek to inquire beyond what Scripture teaches on this matter. So also Sirach warns (3:22, 24): *Seek not out things that are too hard for thee, neither search the things that are above thy strength. But what is commanded thee, think thereupon with reverence, for it is not needful for thee to see with thine eyes the things that are in secret.*

If any *curious head* should demand: *what did God do before he made heaven and earth?* — or *did anything come into his mind to make, when he had never made anything?* And if a *jesting head* (to whom Augustine likens) should reply: *Truly, God was preparing hell for those who ask idle questions* — Augustine himself repudiates this jest. *This I do not answer* (he says); *I would rather answer, what I do not know, than what would make him mocked, who asked a difficult question, and by which one giving a false answer would be praised. But I say: OUR God, Creator of every creature. And if by the name of heaven and earth all creatures are understood, boldly I say: before God made heaven and earth, HE MADE NOTHING* (*Confessions* xi, c. 12).

With this presupposition, nothing in Scripture forbids us to inquire; nor was God idle before the creation, since Scripture does not conceal what he did from eternity, if we consider well: *namely he decreed the goods of the faithful and the punishments of the unfaithful from eternity*. So the Son from eternity he begat, from eternity the Son was begotten, from eternity the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father and the Son, but this internally by an eternal act — and hence he wills that on the seventh day he ceased from all his works (not from any weariness of powers, nor from any total cessation of act, but from *this new sort of work by which he had created things new* — since even in his very rest he speaks his own work, *which the Father hitherto works, and I work* — John 5:17); *the Word of the Lord established the heavens, and by the Spirit of his mouth all the host of them* (Ps 33:6). And *there are certain things which are attributed to God, as when it is said, God rested on the seventh day* (Gen 2:2) — where God is said neither to have needed rest, nor to have any moment yet in the contemplation of his works, so that in this way many things may be attributed to God.

### Case II. Is it truly to be believed that God brought forth all species of things at the first six days, and not before

**Respondeo.** *Augustine* wrestled with this doubt when, *fixing that God created all things simultaneously* — as *if the omnipotent Creator of all things* — is said to have first created the *seeds* or *seminal reasons* of all things: he cites this from Ecclesiasticus (18:1): *He that liveth for ever created all things at once* (see Ambrose in *Hexaemeron*).

Certain animals whose growth follows from certain plants have been produced in the six first days — not because vintage-yield had preceded, nor because plants grew in one day, nor because bees had already made honey. And generally, all things which do not have a *seminal principle*: neither are they from seeds, but by the concurrence of other natural causes as by accident, and thus are said to be in their principles created (see the philosophy of Francis Vallesius, i, c. 2, p. 27).

Today God still operates when he preserves and governs those species in their individuals. Which is expressly attested by Scripture (Jer 12:9): *One Lord, the whole all of Sabbaths — nothing without him*; *whence Jehovah Guardian of Israel does not sleep nor slumber* (Ps 121:4). And Christ himself says the Father works still, whose operation cannot be understood except as the sustenance of nature and the extension of divine action: *my Father worketh hitherto, and I work* (John 5:17). Hence the sixth doctrine of *the work of God ad extra* has been rightly framed: *whatever God ad extra does, he does through the Word, and the Word through the Holy Spirit*.

**We conclude therefore that at the first six days of creation, God made all species of things at once** — but he still operates today in preserving and governing individuals through their species.

### Case III. Whence sin, if God is the author of all things created good

**Respondeo.** The Manichees of old imagined two Gods: *good*, from whom every good in the world came; *evil*, from whom every evil.

Others held that *our flesh is evil*, and the cause of evil; others that of both good and evil compounded, and that then a man sins when he *conforms nature to Deus* — whom Augustine refutes (*De ecclesiae dogmatibus* c. 60 and *Ad Simplicium* i, q. 6).

Also the *Calvinists* have introduced God as the author of the origin of sin. For Calvin himself (*Institutes* i, c. 18) *strongly attributes original sin to the will and providence of God, so that not only did the Fall happen, but God willed and moved the will of Adam to fall*; and elsewhere (*Institutes* iii, c. 23) he speaks of *the ruin of the impious* saying: *If God rules over all, of which he did not first know, nothing can turn out except by his will — even the same sin is not preserved to be against his will.*

Against these assertions it is to be held that *sin is in no way from God, neither by his willing, nor by his approving, nor by his operating, nor by his aiding, nor by his silent instigation of the minds and wills of men*. For the divine is *nature not willing iniquity* (Ps 5:5, Sir 15:11). And with respect to the *work of creation*, Scripture longissime removes it: *God saw everything that he had made, and behold, it was very good* (Gen 1:31). *God made man upright* (Eccl 7:29); *God commanded no man to act impiously, and to no man gave he the spirit of sinning; he desires not a multitude of unfaithful children*, etc. (Sir 15:12, 20).

But **whence then is sin, in this good creation of God? The Scripture has taught in many places** — and especially in the fall of first parents, having shown that: through the devil's proposition and persuasion of evil, or by man's own inclination toward evil. For the first man was created with *free will* — that is, *with the power of sinning and of not sinning* — and when he was inclined to obey by the deception of the devil, having fallen from God, he lost the liberty of doing good, and made nature-prone-to-sin in his posterity, whence man is *born a sinner*, as it is written (Gen 6:5, 8:21): *every imagination of the thoughts of man's heart is only evil continually*, and *by one man sin entered into the world, and by sin death* (Rom 5:12).

*Four modes of the Devil's tempting*: (1) *internally*, by inflaming lust; (2) *by object*, by proposing the object; (3) *occasionally*, by giving occasion; (4) *impulsively*, by persuading and moving the mind toward evil.

Then the *devil and man's malice* alone are the causes of sin, of which nothing is attributed to God — for *sin is not a substance, but a defect and departure from the good creation of God, brought in by the devil*.

### Case IV. Why did not God impede the fall of the first-formed man, seeing he could have created him so that he *could not sin*

**Respondeo.** *Vain is this scrutiny.* For, however we regard the matter, God most-just did well when he did not impede the fall of man — although not in the mode which appears to us. He gave to the first-formed the law he desired to be observed, and appointed the object, and neither by any necessity was he to sin.

Then, when he made man in his own image in original justice, he bestowed on him full powers for the performing of every office to God — namely, an intellect fully knowing the mind of the law, and a will fully able to obey; and he added the threats of most grave punishments by which to draw the will of man, prone toward evil, back. Whence Sirach says of man before the fall (15:14): *He added his commandments and precepts. If thou wilt observe the commandments, they shall preserve thee.*

These impediments in the created righteousness were far more strong to draw back the will from sin than any external violence would have been. For nothing more could have averted a free creature from sinning than perceiving that God, whom he was to obey, would be *incorruptibly severe*.

But why then did God not so constitute man that he *could not* sin — since God created him a rational nature? Because — as Augustine says (*De correptione et gratia* c. 11) — *Adam yielded, when to Adam who could act well as he willed, it was not conferred to be able to persist by liberty of his own will*.

But three reasons can be given why God did not create man such that he *could not* sin:

**1.** God *never determines to make man such that he cannot sin*, since he wills that he act in freedom — not necessarily. *He appointed the law, in whose transgression, however light, at once God's judgment is announced.*

**2.** *Because he could not justly punish a sin except one committed with will*: for a will that sins is what is punished — a reasoning Augustine gives (*De Genesi ad literam* xi, c. 7). But God threatened punishment: therefore the law he gave was such that man was to be by nature able to sin, so that he could deserve *any punishment for it*.

**3.** *Because this very condition tends to the greater glory of man* — for as *voluntary action is more agreeable and more honorable than compelled* by nature, so *spontaneous obedience of a creature is more pleasing to God, and more glorious to man, than one bent by violence.* Therefore Augustine well says: *Just to render obedience is spontaneous, if you are made by nature, so that you cannot transgress.*

Let us spare ourselves therefore in such thoughts, and not draw our Creator into judgment or seek to have him accountable to us. For the wisdom of God our Creator is far greater than that it should be either investigated by us or called into doubt by us.

### Case V. When the works of God relate to Providence, is it to be believed that God's providence extends to *all things*

**Respondeo.** Most certain it is, and every pious man's faith undoubted, that God is universal *arbiter of all things* — not only those which are, but which he begot in the world by his providence. Impossible it is that God should abandon his work. Whence Chrysostom (Homily 19 on Ephesians): *A ship, however firm and instructed, cannot stand without a helmsman; the world without the government of God is that neither one house nor state can stand without governance — so much less could the world*.

To this pertains: *God makes an even path* (Ps 66:7); *he inspects the world* and all who are under heaven (Job 28:24, 34:21); *and by counsel and providence he governs all things* (Wisd 8:1, Sir 24:8).

And though no pious man doubts the universal providence of things, there are yet some who call it in doubt in whole or in part, and by this reasoning render simple souls dubious.

**Cicero** thought it *ridiculous* to attribute care of human affairs to God, whatever is highest, since it is polluted with sad and multiplex ministry (*De natura deorum* i, c. 7). But neither is God ministered to nor contaminated by his providence — nay, is human ordinances just — as neither is his presence, since without any affection and mutation of himself he knows and provides for all: *No task is difficult to God*, says Nazianzen.

Others object to *perverse and disturbed order of things* — which they perceive in the world, and where the wicked are honored, but of these see later in Book II Cap. VI Casus 3.

Then there are those who indeed do not remove all divine providence but negate it in part. **Some think God cares only for celestial things but exclude sublunary things from his providence** — against whom Augustine (on Ps 148) elegantly rebuts: *The land of a storm is troubled by the accidents of every case; and yet in the Psalm hail, fire, snow, mountains, tempests obey the Word of God* — from which he concludes that the order of these things is also subject to divine order.

**Others exempt certain sublunary matters from Divine providence, as those minute and pestiferous little animals — worms, gnats, fleas, flies, worms of the sea, and multitudes of fish, mites, midges, insects.** So *Jerome* takes those things from providence (*Commentary on Habakkuk* i). To these are opposed: *flies and lice in Egypt, which without God's providence were not produced* (Exod 8:18); *the quality of ants, worms and bees* (Sir 11:3, Prov 6:6–8, 30:24–28). If any of these should be foreign to God, then it would not be foreign for him to have care of them (Job 39:36, Deut 32:11).

**Others** — while conceding that God knows the number of the very smallest things — deny that he ordained them, so that certain things happen by chance. Among them is Aristotle (i.13 on *Physics* c. 4) and Averroes.

But Scripture testifies that *God has disposed all things in number* (Wisd 11:21) and that even *the hairs of our head are numbered* (Matt 10:30). Hence Sirach (39:26): *Whatever is under heaven flees not God, but through him is providence; nothing at all escapes his notice — Jehovah both weighs, and imposes on it habitude, number, and quantity, and always accepts it.*

So then of the providence of God is to be thought and spoken, that we neither restrict nor exempt any actions from him. For as much as here is detracted from the providence of God, so much departs from his majesty. But how it is particularly occupied with individuals is not the place here to explain, since I have said it at sufficient length in the *Disputation on Providence*, thesis 76.

### Case VI. To the same end pertains the question, whence one can be certain that no other should be regarded as the promised redeemer, than Jesus of Nazareth

**Respondeo.** Many things there are which can render this faith doubtful — especially the base condition of Jesus of Nazareth in the world; his very tenuous person, the abject servant-form; whereas that work — than which nothing more excellent is in the world — required a person far more excellent.

But **fundamentals of most solid doctrine can be brought forth**, which convince that no other Redeemer is to be looked for:

**1.** First to be borne in mind for us always are the words of the angel who spoke this very counsel to Mary before the birth of the Savior: *thou shalt call his name Jesus, for he shall save his people from their sins* (Matt 1:21). Then again the angel repeated in the night of the nativity, saying to the Shepherds: *Unto you is born this day a Saviour, which is Christ the Lord* (Luke 2:11).

**2.** Then memorable is the sentence of *the Patriarch Jacob* (Gen 49:10): *the sceptre shall not depart from Judah, until Shiloh come; and unto him shall the gathering of the people be.* By *Shiloh* is meant *Jesus of Nazareth*, of whom the angel to Mary said: *he shall be great* (Luke 1:32; cf. Zech 9:9): *thy king cometh unto thee: he is just, and having salvation.* Jesus to himself has applied this (John 12:15).

**3.** *A shepherd rescuing sheep from wolves' jaws — whence Ecclesiasticus 18* it is said: *the shepherd redeemed the sheep, etc.* Daniel 9:14 promises Christ, in whose time sin should be taken away, and inequities blotted out, and everlasting righteousness brought in (Dan 9:24). *Have mercy upon me, O Lord, and save me in the Lord thy God — that is, in Jesus of Nazareth*, of whom the angel to Mary said: *thou shalt call his name Jesus* (Luke 1:31; Zech 9:9); *for he shall save his people from their sins* (Matt 1:21).

**4.** *These also are added other testimonies of the New Testament*: as *Christ was born and appeared* (Luke 19:10, where he speaks of himself: *the Son of Man came to seek and to save that which was lost*; 1 Tim 1:15) — which Peter testifies: *by the precious blood of Christ the Son of God we are redeemed from our sins* (1 Pet 1:18–19; 1 John 1:7).

**By these and similar things — of which Scripture is full — the conscience can be confirmed, that we need not seek any other Redeemer than Jesus, Mary's Son, for our redemption and salvation.**

Nor does the abject condition of his coming into the world hinder — for our salvation is not to be estimated according to our judgments, but according to the counsel of God. When by such contempt of men his coming was rendered great in the face of God, in whom he was well-pleased (Matt 3:17; 17:5), we too are pleased, and by this we approach God through him (Eph 3:12).

For Christ has a double **ornament**, which the eyes of the faithful only can behold: one is *natural* — the sanctity of his innocence itself, for he is *separated from sinners* (Heb 7:26; Isa 53:9). The other is *personal* — because his human nature, assumed into the person of the Son of God, becomes part of the most sacred Trinity, is filled with every majesty, glory, and power of the DEITY, so that *he is even adored by angels* (Heb 1:6), and *is the fairest among the sons of men* (Ps 45:2).

Whom therefore the *vileness* of his condition shows, this his very ornament also demonstrates.

### Case VII. But how can we be profited by another's redemption — since God commands: *whoever has sinned, the same shall die*

**Respondeo.** By this objection the Photinians shake tender consciences. But first of all it must be firmly held that *the work of our redemption depends on the arbitration of God*, to whom we ought not to prescribe by what mode he wills to procure it, or ought. Nor may we owe it to Christ (Eph 1); if therefore it has pleased Him, we can be redeemed by another's satisfaction: who art thou, that repliest to God — why do you not gratefully receive this mercy of God by simple faith?

Openly, however, Scripture testifies that we are redeemed by another's price. Just as Paul writes (Rom 5:19): *For as by one man's disobedience many were made sinners, so by the obedience of one shall many be made righteous.*

**2.** We are redeemed by the price of Christ; for he himself says: *He solved what he had not seized* (Ps 69:4); *he gave his life for us* (Matt 20:28); *the Father gave the Son for us* (John 3:16); *the Son delivered himself for us* (Eph 5:2). Christ *died for all* (2 Cor 5:15), and *the blood of Christ cleanses us from sins* (1 John 1:7); and *the blood of Christ, who through the eternal Spirit offered himself without spot to God, cleanses our conscience from dead works* (Heb 9:14). Peter also expressly writes: *we are redeemed with the precious blood of Christ* (1 Pet 1:19).

Ezekiel's words should not be opposed to this — for it cannot happen that the Holy Spirit contradict himself. **Ezekiel's words** treat of the law which God gave men not for himself, but for human courts: God so wills that this be observed, that the father shall not be plagued for the son, nor the son for the father, nor any innocent person for another — but each shall receive according to his own works, as it is written: *the fathers shall not die for the children, nor the children for the fathers, but each shall die for his own sin* (Deut 24:16); on which account *Amaziah the king of Judah*, when he demanded punishment from his servants who had killed his father, spared their sons (2 Kgs 14:6).

But to this law God did not bind himself. Therefore in the counsel of punishing men's sins, for his supreme mercy, in order to spare the servant, he *did not spare his Son*. In which kind of the counsel of redeeming, it is simply the will of him who counsels — which we must admire with grateful minds, not scrupulously dispute.

**Third — a distinction is to be made here between law and gospel.** If God had wished to act with us according to law, no one would be saved: to the gospel pertains our redemption, where the *epikeia* of God finds place — who, in order to bestow mercy on us and free us from sins, offered up justice in his Son, upon whom he laid the sins of all of us, together with *the chastisement of our peace* (Isa 53:5). This is what Paul says (2 Cor 5:21): *For he hath made him to be sin for us, who knew no sin; that we might be made the righteousness of God in him.*

This is that admirable tempering of law and gospel, justice and mercy, which exceeds every intellect of ours — but in the work of our redemption easily removes difficulty for the faithful.

### Case VIII. But the soul is not yet satisfied and searches: how could God accept the blood of the human man Christ for the price of our redemption, when he elsewhere forbade the shedding of human blood

**Respondeo.** This scrutiny made *Peter Vallardus* a heretic, who would not believe that human blood could restore life to the world (as in Bernard *Epistle* 130 to Innocent). Better therefore would it be to marvel at this counsel of God than to scrutinize it, and to have faith in those Scriptures which testify to this expressly.

**Great is here the cloud of witnesses** — like what the Patriarch Jacob predicted of the Messiah (Gen 49:11): *he shall wash his garments in wine, and his clothes in the blood of grapes* — that he is said to be *wounded for our transgressions* (Isa 53:5); that *the winepress he trod alone, and his vestment was sprinkled with blood* (Isa 63:3).

In the New Testament it is clearer still, when the Apostles write: *whom God hath set forth to be a propitiation through faith in his blood* (Rom 3:25); *being now justified by his blood* (Rom 5:9); *the blood of Jesus his Son cleanseth us from all sin* (1 John 1:7); *the blood of Christ cleanses conscience from dead works* (Heb 9:14). And in general: *without shedding of blood there is no remission* (Heb 9:22).

If these such clear testimonies of Scripture do not yet satisfy, let one further consider four reasons:

**1.** *First is the will of God*, whose counsel of our redemption depended on his most gracious pleasure, since (as Bernard says) *rather he willed by blood than by word*, so that the more he would show us his love, and by his blood he might have his own united with him.

**2.** *The other is the truth of divine justice*. To the first man from the forbidden tree it was threatened *thou shalt die* (Gen 2:17), which death our first parents by the transgression of the mandate deserved. Since therefore the Son of God freely undertook the malediction of the law upon himself, and for that cause was to satisfy the law for us in place of the man-mother, therefore he was to die and his blood to be shed.

**3.** *The third is the end of his incarnation.* The Son of Man came, that he might take away our sins, and destroy the works of the devil (1 John 3:8); and this had to be done by suffering and dying (Heb 2:14). Thus he willed to give himself for us, so that he assumed for us not shed blood in things which for others were forbidden. That which concerns the *shedding of blood* of one — *when magistrates shed the blood of a murderer, they do a thing acceptable to God, because they act according to God's will* (Gen 9:6) — much more grateful to the eternal Father is the shedding of the blood of his beloved Son; whom he *willed, decreed, counseled* — for our sins to be poured out.

### Case IX. Does the Holy Spirit sanctify infants of Christians in the mothers' womb, before they are born

**Respondeo.** By this question the *Calvinist* spirit troubles consciences. For since they enervate the efficacy of baptism (in which they say no one is regenerated, but they hold regeneration to depend from elsewhere), they attribute to the infants of holy parents a natural sanctity in which they are born by force of divine promise: *I will be their God and their seed's*.

Therefore *Beza* (*Ep.* 22 to *Responder-fool sacred sordid error* — he calls the beginning of our salvation *from baptism* — Part 2, *Reg. ad Anatolic coll.* p. 133) writes many things; especially: *he confesses the first step to salvation to be, that in the Church, children are born of pious parents, but not that they are baptized.* Where among others he writes thus: *We concede that faith intervenes, that we should please God through the Covenant — infants indeed; but we deny that they are endowed with the *right* by which we adults are, since we cannot lead this faith on our own; nay, indeed, the *eternal life* is bound to those in the Covenant, from birth from pious parents, and if it could have been, it was to be sealed in them by baptism afterward.*

So Calvin (*Instit.* iv, c. 15, s. 20): *Our infants before they are born God pronounces to be adopted into his own; when he promises to us that he will be our God, and to our seed after us: in this word their salvation is contained. This promise suffices, etc.* *Zanchius* (in *Confessio* p. 163): *the children of the faithful are therefore preserved, because they are in the covenant of God and holy; but adults, because they believe truly in Christ.*

**Whence some Fathers of the Church call the infants Christians before baptism, because they are born of Christians** (see the 11th chapter of Rom., dub. 12; earlier 1 Cor 7:14).

From all these follows that the *infants of Christians before baptism have faith*, please God, are holy sons of God and heirs of eternal life — since no one is in the covenant of God who is not the Son of God, who does not have faith, who does not please God — all which, since they are the works of the Holy Spirit, follows that infants of this kind are *filled with the Holy Spirit before baptism*.

**But if these things are true, it is necessary that baptism is conferred on them in vain**, especially since *Calvin* expressly writes that the promise of the covenant suffices for them; whence he denies that *baptism is absolutely necessary for salvation* (Beza *Resp. 2 ad Anatol. Colloquy* p. 158).

**Which doctrine is far most pernicious**, introducing contempt of the sacraments and manifestly contradicting the Holy Spirit. For Scripture doctors: *that which is born of the flesh is flesh* (John 3:6); that *we are all by nature children of wrath* (Eph 2:3); *that unless one is born again of water and the Spirit, he cannot enter the kingdom of God* (John 3:5); *for as many as received him, to them gave he power to become the sons of God, which were born, not of blood, nor of the will of the flesh, nor of the will of man, but of God* (John 1:12–13).

Not to natural birth but to *baptism* is ascribed the sanctification and regeneration of man, on which account it is called *the laver of regeneration* (Titus 3:5). And Christ himself is said *to cleanse the Church by the washing of water by the word* (Eph 5:26). Whence Tertullian rightly said (*Apol.* c. 16): *Christians are not born, but made.* *Baptism is the workshop of Christians* — *regeneration, not generation, makes Christians*, as Augustine speaks (*De peccat. merit. et remissione* i, c. 9). In baptism, then, in the ordinary way, the infants of Christians are filled with the Holy Spirit, who does not enter a malignant soul (as is in all not yet regenerate, whom Paul calls *sons of wrath*), with whom the Spirit of grace can have no commerce.

Which truth is so clear that it *pierced even the eyes of Rodolphus Gualtherus* (Calvinist), who in Homily 3 on Acts writes: *why should carnal generation contribute to salvation, since it is polluted by the corruption of nature we have from our first parent, or by hereditary right transfused into us? Certainly of that David spoke (Ps 51:5): behold, in iniquity was I fashioned, and in sins did my mother conceive me. Sinners therefore are born, and not sons of adoption, before they are consecrated to Christ and the heavenly Kingdom.* And *Tertullian* also rightly said: *Christians are not born but made.*

**But since in the promise it is said** *I will be their God and their seed's*, it is not to be understood absolutely, but with a *determination of faith*, promising to Christians in the interpretation of the Apostle (Gal 3:7): *they who are of faith are sons of Abraham* (Rom 9:6): *for they are not all Israel, which are of Israel; neither, because they are the seed of Abraham, are they all children: but, in Isaac shall thy seed be called* (v. 7).

Since therefore this way the mind is confirmed regarding plurality in God, and the plurality is precisely three, not more or fewer — the third thing is to be shown: *these three are not three gods, but three distinct persons.*

To what else that the *root be holy, so are the branches* (Rom 11:16), and *the children are holy* (1 Cor 7:14) — this does not have the sense that the sons of holy parents are born the sons of faith and in the covenant of God — but *legal sanctity* is meant, because they have the right and privilege of consequently obtaining the goods of the Church, from which those born outside are alien, until through legitimate means the Lord calls them too. On which account Augustine interprets that sanctity, of which the Apostle speaks, not as sanctity of faith but as *communion of the sacraments and likeness of profession* (Ep. 108).

**We conclude therefore that the Holy Spirit does not yet regenerate or sanctify the children of the most holy parents, until through baptism they are regenerated and made sons: for *whom the Spirit of God rules, they are the sons of God* (Rom 8:14).**

Commended to us then by the Apostle as it were the *cup of grace comforts* — this comforts the pious parents whose infants are extinguished in the womb, or before they can be baptized, if with prayers they commend them to God, they need not doubt of their salvation: since *the lack of the sacrament, not the contempt of the sacrament, condemns*. Hence Gerson (*Ad partem cancellar.*, in the sermon on the Nativity of the Virgin Mary) writes: *God has thus bound his mercy to the sacraments of salvation, that not without the prejudice of the law he could sanctify children not yet born outside the uterus by his grace of baptism, or by the power of the Holy Spirit — and so he admonishes parents to pray for the infant not yet born, that they should not fear that all hope of salvation is taken away from those who go without baptism.*

— ❦ —

## Chapter V. On Cases of Conscience Concerning Religion and the Internal Cult of God

*De casibus conscientiae circa religionem cultumque Dei internum.*

**Religion** is taken in two modes: (1) of the *moral virtue*, which gives to God the just and due honor. (2) of a certain *state and condition of the life* of many men, who by a special mode and rule think they worship God. In the first signification here it is considered; in the second below it will have its own peculiar place.

Religion, therefore, as it is a *moral virtue*, is defined by *Augustine* (*De vera religione* c. 1) — by which term the true worship of God is expressed in Scripture (1 Tim 1:6, 3:16; Titus 1:1; 1 Pet 1). Otherwise it is called *piety*; and by reason of etymology it is distinguished from theology, in that theology consists in *knowledge*, while religion or piety consists in *performance and work*. But *as to the thing itself*, religion embraces both — namely the knowledge of God or wisdom, and the performance or cult of God.

For as Lactantius writes (*Institutes* iv, c. 4): *religion cannot be separated from wisdom, nor wisdom from religion: it is one and the same God, who both must be understood (which is of wisdom) and honored (which is of religion). But wisdom precedes, religion follows: because to know God is prior; to worship God is consequent.*

Hence it stands that there are two heads of religion: first, the *acknowledgment of God*; the other, the *worship of God* — which Solomon conjoins (1 Chr 28:9): *Thou also, Solomon my son, acknowledge the God of thy father, and worship him with an integral heart and a willing mind.* For only he is a *servant* who does, not only who understands (Matt 7:26; Luke 12:47). *He who says, I know him, and keeps not his commandments, is a liar* (1 John 2:4).

The cases which fall around the *knowledge of God* we have seen hitherto. Now the worship of God follows. Here the cult is either *true and legitimate*, which is prescribed in the Word and is properly called *religion*; or *false*, and *devised by men in the confidence of their own wits* — and this is called *superstition* (as *Lactantius* writes iv, c. 28): *this it is which separates religion from superstition — that religion is true worship, superstition false*.

**True worship of God is twofold: one *internal*, the other *external*** — which are called by others two acts of religion. About each various cases can occur in conscience. Concerning the former in this chapter we treat; the latter we reserve for the following.

### Case I. Above all the pious mind wishes to be certain — what is the internal worship of God, and what things are required for it, lest by omission of some part he unwittingly fall short of the true worship of God

**Respondeo.** The internal worship of God has its seat in the mind of man, and is the foundation of every external reverence and honor which is exhibited to God by words, works, and gestures of man. *God looks upon the heart* (1 Sam 16:7). And the true *worshippers worship the Father in Spirit and in truth* (John 4:23).

Two are the acts of internal worship: *devotion* and *submission*.

**Devotion** is when a man directs all the thoughts of his heart, the desires of his will, motions and affections, to God alone — clings wholly to him, depends on him alone, and yields himself to his beckoning. Hence Scripture joins with the worship of God adherence to him: *Thou shalt fear the Lord thy God; thou shalt worship him; thou shalt cleave to him* (Deut 10:20). Elsewhere it is called the *devotion of the mind, inquisition of God, and approach to God*: *The Lord is with you while ye be with him; if ye seek him, he will be found of you* (2 Chr 15:2); *draw nigh to God, and he will draw nigh to you* (James 4:8).

And this devotion is the highest joy of the pious: hence David says (Ps 73:28): *it is good for me to draw nigh to God: I have put my trust in the Lord God*; (Ps 119:31): *I have stuck unto thy testimonies: O Lord, put me not to shame*. Great utility, and divine blessing, does he confer: for *Lord*, thou art *good and forgiving, and plenteous in mercy unto all them that call upon thee* (Ps 86:5); *he will fulfill the desire of them that fear him* (Ps 145:19).

This devotion of mind embraces three cardinal virtues — of **faith, hope, love**.

- **Faith** is that virtue by which the pious one relies on the Word of God about a rectified reason and promise of divine help, and by which we are drawn from anxieties of the world, from infirmities of the flesh, to look up alone to celestial help. Whence Abraham, robustly by faith against hope in the truth of divine promise, in the multiplication of his seed *believed* (Rom 4:18). This is true security of the pious: for while others in adversities seek escape by other means in vain, they by consideration of God's promises, whom they know is Father and Almighty, sweetly rest. *He who believeth shall not make haste* (Isa 28:16).
- **Hope** is expectation of divine help in time of calamities, when no help either near or far distant appears, and dangers meanwhile increase — yet by continual faith is sustained. *If God be for us, who can be against us?* (Rom 8:31). *The Lord is my light and my salvation, whom shall I fear? The Lord is the strength of my life, of whom shall I be afraid?* (Ps 27:1). *In God have I put my trust: I will not be afraid what man can do unto me* (Ps 56:11). Peculiar to hope is *to await* things not obvious to the eyes, and to bear its delay patiently. For *hope deferred maketh the heart sick* (Prov 13:12), but *hope of the Lord is enduringly firm* — as *the Lord is my hope, in him will I trust* (Ps 91:2). Scripture calls this hope *the anchor of the soul, sure and steadfast* (Heb 6:19), and *the helmet of our salvation* (1 Thess 5:8; Eph 6:17).
- **Charity or love** is that affection of the heart by which God is preferred to all things, and for the sake of God all things are loved. For God himself is our highest good, which the pious desire, to which all things they refer, to which all thoughts they direct, that they may attain it. God demands this love from all: *Thou shalt love the Lord thy God with all thy heart, and with all thy soul, and with all thy might* (Deut 6:5). And this love of God the Apostle judges so necessary that he writes: *If any man love not the Lord Jesus Christ, let him be Anathema Maranatha* (1 Cor 16:22). It arises from consideration of God's love toward us, whence flow innumerable benefits, which move us to love God our so bounteous benefactor. Whence John writes: *Herein is love, not that we loved God, but that he loved us* (1 John 4:10, 19). And this love: (1) makes us rest in the one God alone, and though *all things in the world are great*, we count nothing our chief good save him; (2) makes us despise the insolent tumults of enemies, placing hope in the Lord; (3) seeks communion and quasi-union with him by whatever means we can — for it is the nature of love to desire to be joined to what it loves. Whence Augustine: *the soul is not where it lives, but where it loves*. This union is made *through faith, by which Christ dwells in our hearts* (Eph 3:17); faith is kindled and preserved through the Word and Sacraments — which whoever sincerely loves God, does not neglect but seeks and loves as the one thing necessary, without which there is separation from God. (4) It makes us conform our whole life, so far as may be, to God's beckoning and will — for we do not willingly offend whom we love. Hence Christ says: *he that hath my commandments, and keepeth them, he it is that loveth me* (John 14:21).
